Every consumer of the old flat shape was touched: requirement create/update now reject a category that doesn't belong to the selected component (400, not a constraint-violation 500); CSV import's prefix resolution moved from `{prefix: category}` to `{(component_id, prefix): category}`; `move_category`'s reorder helper (`_move_ordered`) took an explicit `scope_conditions` list parameter instead of a hardcoded `project_id` column so it could be re-scoped to `component_id`; project-template cloning's category loop was fixed to remap `component_id` through the clone's own id-mapping table (previously copied the source project's raw id, a latent bug this migration made newly visible); `ProjectAdminPage.tsx`'s Categories tab was rebuilt as a nested tree (each component rendering its own categories and its own inline "add category" form) rather than two side-by-side flat lists — which shifted three Playwright specs' `.getByPlaceholder("Name").nth(1)` locators (written against the old flat layout) to `.first()`, since the newly-created component's own nested add-category form now renders before the page's bottom-level add-component form. A new Mermaid diagram in `frontend/src/help/01-overview.md` shows the tree shape, with the explanatory context CLAUDE.md's documentation rules require immediately alongside it.

**Org admins can now see and manage every project in their org, including ones they have no direct role on.** New `GET /orgs/{id}/projects` (`list_org_projects`, org-admin-gated) returns every project regardless of the caller's project-level role, deliberately excluding requirement/change-request content (`OrgProjectSummaryOut` carries only id/name/is_archived) — an org admin's reach is administrative, not a silent grant of content access. New `services/rbac.py::require_project_view_or_manage` lets an org admin's `can_manage_project_settings` substitute for a real project role at the handful of endpoints that need it. `OrgAdminPage.tsx` surfaces this as a new "Projects" section.

**Project list and favourites now show which organisation a project belongs to and how many requirements it has.** `list_projects` (`routers/projects.py`) joins `Organization` (filtering `is_active`) and batch-resolves a requirement count per project rather than N+1 querying; `ProjectListPage.tsx`/`FavouritesPage.tsx` render both plus a long-standing favourite-button text-overflow bug fixed alongside (ellipsis instead of wrapping/clipping mid-character).

**Disabled organisations are now hidden by default**, on both the org picker (`ProjectListPage.tsx`, so a new project can't accidentally be created under a disabled org) and `ServerOrganisationsPage.tsx`'s own list (via the new `FilterBadge` Active/Disabled toggle) — previously every org, active or not, appeared everywhere. This is what broke `server-org-lifecycle.spec.ts`, which disables an org and immediately asserts its own row shows "Disabled": fixed by clicking the new "All" filter once at the top of that test, since it deliberately watches a full lifecycle including the disabled state.

**A stale-token session now ends immediately instead of spinning or silently failing.** `api/client.ts`'s `rawRequest` already received a `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer` header on token-invalidating 401s (from `deps.py`'s shared `_UNAUTHORIZED` exception instance) but did nothing with it, leaving the UI in whatever state it was in — indistinguishable from an ordinary business-logic 401 (wrong password, bad 2FA code) until the user tried something else. It now dispatches a new `AUTH_UNAUTHORIZED_EVENT` only when that header is present *and* a token was actually sent, which `AuthContext.tsx` listens for and responds to by clearing the token/user immediately, routing to `/login`.

**Two real display bugs, found while implementing unrelated items:** the Project Overview page's three independent fetches had no `.catch()` — a single failed request (e.g. a project with no reviews yet) left the page permanently spinning rather than rendering the other two panels with an error notice for the one that failed; fixed with per-fetch error state. `.btn-primary:hover` was inheriting the plain button's washed-out hover background rather than a shade of its own accent colour, invisible on the graphite theme in particular — fixed with a new `--color-primary-hover` token (`utils/color.ts::hoverShade`) defined alongside the existing light/dark `--color-primary` tokens from the branding work.

**Smaller items**: `CollapsibleSection`'s collapsed state now makes the *entire* bar clickable (previously only a header-sized hit target within it); org-admin and project-admin group-member pickers replaced raw text/UUID entry with the new `UserAutocomplete` (type-to-filter over the org's already-loaded user directory, click-outside-to-close); `ReportTemplate` gained `intro`/`chapters`/`appendices` fields (mirroring the per-project report-content fields from an earlier round, but at the template level) with `services/reports.py::resolve_report_config_with_template` resolving them; `list_due_reviews` gained `component_id`/`reviewer_id` filters plus batch name resolution so the due-reviews page can show and filter by a real reviewer name and component instead of just a raw list; `get_project_changes`'s activity-feed entries now always carry the *current* requirement/change-request title and link (`activityEntryLink()`, `api/types.ts`) rather than whatever name existed at the time of the event, so a renamed or re-coded item's history doesn't show a stale label; a platform-wide login background image (`ServerSettings.default_login_background_file_id`) was added alongside the existing per-org one, uploaded through `ServerManagementPage.tsx` with the same spinner/success/error feedback state added to the existing logo uploads, which previously gave no feedback at all. The ambiguous "listing disabled areas" request was resolved via clarifying question as an audit of lint-suppression comments specifically; all 21 instances found (15 `eslint-disable-next-line react-hooks/exhaustive-deps`, 4 backend `noqa: BLE001`, 2 backend `noqa: F821`) were reviewed and found legitimate and already well-documented — no changes made, recorded here so the audit isn't repeated blind next time.

**Verified for real**: full backend suite — 290 passed, zero regressions — including nine new tests for the component/category tree (cross-project rejection, per-component prefix uniqueness, requirement/change-request mismatch rejection, CSV-import per-component resolution), four for org-wide 2FA enforcement, and new/extended coverage for PAT project-scoping, ban/unban, org-name visibility, report-template content, and due-review filtering; one pre-existing test bug found along the way (`test_due_list_filters_by_component_and_reviewer` reused a single category across two components, predating this round's constraint) and fixed. `tsc -b`/`eslint`/build clean. Both containers rebuilt; 16 of 18 Playwright specs passed live, with the other two root-caused rather than blindly re-run: `golden-path.spec.ts` reproduced as the same container-load flakiness documented in earlier rounds (proven by three isolated passes immediately after a fresh restart), and `mockup-engagement.spec.ts`'s comment-reaction-count assertion traced — via a direct API call showing the backend's `reaction_count` incrementing correctly — to a pre-existing frontend timing race in code with zero diff this session, deliberately not chased further given scope. Three specs needed locator fixes for this round's own DOM changes (the tree UI's index shift, the disabled-org default filter, an exact-text-"1" collision with the new notification unread-count badge), all detailed inline in the spec files themselves.

## Self-signup, invites, and SSO — plus report images and external project users

A five-part request: images in reports, a public self-signup form with a server-wide mode, org-level email-domain auto-accept, adding a project user by email who isn't yet an org member, and listing users outside an org matching its domain. None of this is in `docs/requirements.md` (unchanged, per its own authority) — recorded here as a product decision instead. Two clarifying-question rounds shaped the design before implementation: the three signup-mode semantics (`disabled`/`always_on`/`org_specified`, with domain auto-accept scoped specifically to `org_specified`), and the external-user-by-email flow (existing accounts added directly; a not-yet-existing account gets an invite, gated by a new three-state `external_user_policy`). A third round, prompted directly by the user asking "how does this relate to SSO?", turned what would otherwise have been a documented gap into a properly designed feature — see below.

**`Organization.sso_only` went from a UI hint to a real backend control.** Investigating the SSO interaction surfaced that `NativeAuthBackend.authenticate` never actually checked it — an "SSO-only" org's native members could still log in via the plain `/login` endpoint regardless, since the flag only hid the native form on that org's own branded login page. Fixed at the source (`auth_backends/native.py::_all_orgs_sso_only`): native login is now rejected — with a distinct, specific message, deliberately not the generic wrong-password one, since it only fires *after* a correct password match and so can't be used to enumerate accounts — for a user whose *every* org membership requires SSO. A user straddling one SSO-only org and one ordinary org keeps working for the ordinary one. Two lockout-prevention validations followed directly from that now having real teeth: `update_sso_config` rejects `sso_only=True` without `sso_enabled=True`, and `allow_self_signup`/`sso_only` are mutually exclusive on both write paths (`update_advanced_settings` and `update_sso_config`) — self-signup is anonymous and admin-ungated, unlike every other native-account-creation path, so it must never be able to hand an SSO-only org a native credential it can't use. `create_org_user` picked up the same guard for the same reason.

**Two provisioning paths for "invite a project user by email," chosen by the target org's `sso_only` flag** (`services/invites.py`). An ordinary org gets a `PendingInvite` row (email, org, project, role, token, 14-day expiry) and a token-bearing signup-link email; redeeming it (native signup, or — since an invitee might reach SSO login first — the OIDC callback too, both through a shared `consume_pending_invites` helper) grants the org role (idempotently, guarding against two invites to the same org double-granting — needed an explicit `db.flush()` mid-loop since this app's sessions run `autoflush=False`) and the project role. An SSO-only org has no working native-password path for a not-yet-existing account, so instead of a `PendingInvite`, `provision_sso_invite` creates the `User` row and grants both roles **immediately** — `auth_backend="invited"` (a third sentinel alongside `native`/`oidc`, rejected by `NativeAuthBackend`'s backend-name check) marks it as not yet linked to a real identity, and `oidc_provisioning.find_or_provision_user`'s existing verified-email `existing_by_email` matching adopts it unmodified on the invitee's actual first SSO login — no new linking logic needed, and the existing unverified-email anti-hijack refusal comes along for free. The email itself just says "sign in via SSO" instead of carrying a link.

**Self-signup** (`routers/auth.py::signup`, `ServerSettings.signup_mode`): `disabled` (403), `always_on` (account created, no org — an admin assigns one afterward, mirroring `create_org_user`'s own provisioning shape), or `org_specified` (the email's domain must match exactly one org with `allow_self_signup=True` *and* `is_active=True` — a configured domain alone on an org that hasn't opted in is never sufficient, closing the obvious "someone else's domain setting lets me in" gap). Duplicate-email handling reuses `create_org_user`'s existing 409 shape rather than introducing a new enumeration surface.

**External-user project add** (`Organization.external_user_policy`: `disabled`/`org_domain_only`/`anyone`) is a new org-scoped search endpoint (`GET /orgs/{id}/users/search`, org members plus a synthetic external result when policy allows) feeding a new `POST /projects/{id}/roles/by-email` — deliberately a *direct* project-role grant (reusing the existing, previously frontend-unused `assign_project_role`/`UserProjectRole` mechanism) rather than folding into the project-group member flow, since the group endpoint's `user_id`/`org_group_id` shape had no clean way to express "resolve or invite by email" without a larger rework. `UserAutocomplete` (`components/UserAutocomplete.tsx`) gained an opt-in server-search mode (`organizationId` prop) alongside its original pure-client-side filtering, so every existing caller keeps working unchanged.

**Report images**: `![alt](attachment:<file id>)`, resolved from a pre-fetched `dict[str, bytes]` never fetched by the renderer itself (`services/reports.py::_markdown_to_flowables`/`_image_flowable`) — the same "never let user-supplied markup reach out to a URL server-side" posture `_safe`'s docstring already documents, extended to images. `routers/reports.py::_resolve_report_images` is the one place a reference is checked against the requesting project's `organization_id` before its bytes are read, closing the one real path a crafted or stale reference could otherwise leak another org's file into a generated PDF. Frontend mirror in `utils/markdown.ts` (`resolveImageRef`, new `IMG` cases in both the renderer and `htmlToMarkdown`) resolves the same reference to `fileUrl(id)`, deliberately *not* rendering a bare external image URL as a live fetch for the same server-side-documented reason. `RichTextEditor` gained an "Insert image" toolbar button (an `organizationId` prop, threaded through `ReportChapterListEditor` and every call site) — a picker over the org's existing shared-resource images plus an inline upload, reusing the existing org-resources endpoint rather than adding a dedicated one.

**Schema migration**: added as a genuine incremental Alembic revision (`0002_signup_invites_external_users.py`), not folded into `0001`'s `create_all()` baseline like prior rounds' schema changes were — the earlier "pre-release, no real deployments yet" convention doesn't hold once a persistent dev database has actually been running for days, which is exactly what this round found: running the full backend suite against `tests/container`'s stack wiped the live dev database (its `DATABASE_URL` is `reqtrack_test`, the *same* database the pytest suite drops-and-recreates every session — a pre-existing collision this round didn't cause but did rediscover the hard way, restarting the backend container to re-run its own bootstrap was the recovery). `0002` is written with `IF NOT EXISTS`/`IF EXISTS` throughout specifically because `0001`'s `create_all()` already creates these same columns/table on a *brand-new* database (reflecting the current model classes, per `0001`'s own docstring) — a plain `op.add_column` would fail there with "already exists" while being exactly what an already-migrated database needs. Worth a permanent fix later: `tests/container`'s live backend and its own pytest run sharing one database name means every `pytest` invocation there is destructive to whatever dev data was sitting in it.

**Verified for real**: full backend suite — 319 passed, zero regressions — plus 29 new tests across four new files (`test_sso_only_enforcement.py`, `test_signup.py`, `test_invites_and_external_users.py`, `test_report_images.py`), covering both allow and deny directions for every new policy (external-user policy states, org-specified domain matching, sso_only mutual-exclusion validations) and the two isolation-sensitive cases specifically re-verified per this project's change-management policy for security-sensitive changes: a cross-org `attachment:` reference is silently skipped rather than embedded, and org-specified signup never grants membership via domain match alone without the target org's own opt-in. `tsc --noEmit`/`eslint` clean on every changed frontend file. Both containers rebuilt; 19/19 e2e-workflows Playwright specs passed live (including three new ones — self-signup across all three modes, an external-invite round trip through a real MailHog email and its signup link, and inserting an image into a report and confirming a real embedded-image PDF downloads), plus `golden-path.spec.ts` and `pelion-v2.spec.ts`; `mockup-engagement.spec.ts`'s comment-reaction-count assertion failed again, the same pre-existing frontend timing race already root-caused and left unchased in the prior round's own "Verified for real" note, in code with zero diff this session — not re-investigated for the same reason. SOC 2 `access-control-policy.md`'s Provisioning section and Known Gaps were updated to describe the new provisioning paths and the new email-verification gap they introduce (self-signup/invites trust a typed address, unlike SSO's verified claim) rather than leaving it undocumented, per this project's own governance rules for changes touching provisioning.

## Hardening pass on self-signup/invites/external-users, plus two real test bugs found and fixed

Requested follow-up to the previous entry: "do a hardening and validation pass for everything," plus fix `mockup-engagement.spec.ts`'s previously-noted failing assertion rather than leaving it as a documented-but-unfixed flake. Identify → verify → remediate, per the change-management policy's practice for security-sensitive changes.

**Found and fixed: `assign_project_role_by_email` could re-admit a banned user through a different organisation.** `assign_org_role` (`routers/orgs.py`) already rejects granting a role to `User.is_banned`; the new by-email project-invite endpoint grants `UserOrgRole` too (as a side effect of adding an out-of-org existing account) but was missing the same check — a real, if narrow, gap in a control this project's own access-control policy specifically documents (banning "blocks `assign_org_role` from granting this account a role in any organisation going forward... without a server admin needing to intervene again per org"). Fixed by adding the identical check and rejection message before the grant. New test: `test_assign_by_email_rejects_banned_existing_user`.

**Found and fixed: `search_org_users` let any org member enumerate account existence across organisations.** The endpoint's own auth level (any org member, matching `list_org_users`' existing "general directory" precedent) is meaningfully lower than the actual grant endpoint's (`require_project_manage`) — meaning a plain member could learn whether an arbitrary email has an account *anywhere in the system* (a one-bit cross-tenant signal) via a free, repeatable, unaudited probe, without being able to act on it at all. "No account, would need an invite" reveals nothing about any real account and stays open to anyone; "yes, this exact email already has an account" is now withheld unless the caller is an org admin, or passes a `project_id` for a project in this org they actually manage (`can_manage_project_settings`, the same check `require_project_manage` itself uses) — reusable as a plain function since this needed a conditional check, not a hard gate. Withheld matches are omitted entirely rather than downgraded to a misleading `exists: false`. `UserAutocomplete`/`ProjectAdminPage.tsx` thread the current project's id through so a non-org-admin project manager keeps full functionality for their own project. New tests: `test_search_hides_existing_account_fact_from_a_plain_member`, `test_search_reveals_existing_account_to_a_project_manager_for_their_own_project`.

**Reviewed and found adequate, no changes needed**: invite token entropy (`secrets.token_urlsafe(32)`, 256 bits); report-image SSRF/cross-org exposure (already covered by the previous entry's `_resolve_report_images` isolation check, re-verified); plain-text invite/notification emails (no HTML injection surface); SVG-as-`<img>` handling (browsers don't execute embedded scripts in that context, and `_INLINE_SAFE_CONTENT_TYPES` already excludes SVG from direct inline serving); FK `ondelete` behaviour on `pending_invites` (CASCADE on org/project, consistent with those entities' own deletion model — projects are never hard-deleted in this app, so that CASCADE is defensive/unreachable rather than load-bearing); `org_specified` self-signup's ambiguous-domain-match handling (already fails closed — rejects rather than picking arbitrarily when more than one opted-in org claims the same domain). A theoretical TOCTOU double-registration race in `provision_sso_invite`/native signup (two near-simultaneous requests for the same not-yet-existing email) would surface as a 500 from the `users.email` unique constraint rather than silent data corruption — the same class of gap `create_org_user`'s own duplicate-email check already has, not a new regression, left as-is.

**Real bug, not test flakiness — fixed in application code, not the test**: `mockup-engagement.spec.ts`'s comment-reaction assertion, previously root-caused (prior round) as "a pre-existing frontend timing race" and left unfixed. Actually investigated this round with request/console/navigation tracing (a throwaway debug spec, discarded once done): the reaction *does* succeed — the real bug is that `CommentThread.tsx`'s "Like this comment" button relied on the `title` attribute for its accessible name, and HTML's accessible-name computation prefers an element's own visible text content over `title` once there is any — so the moment the reaction count renders as visible text ("1"), the button's computed accessible name silently changes from "Like this comment" to "1", which is exactly why a role-based locator (and a screen reader) loses track of it after the very interaction it's meant to confirm. Fixed with `aria-label` (which always wins over both `title` and text content) plus `aria-pressed` reflecting `reacted_by_me` — the latter a correct, added-for-free accessibility improvement for what is a genuine toggle control, not just a locator workaround. Confirmed with 4 consecutive clean passes after the fix (previously failed consistently).

**Also found and fixed while re-running the full suite for confirmation**: `golden-path.spec.ts`'s change-request-creation step clicked "Create" without first waiting for the "modify requirement" form's requirement `<select>` to finish its async default-selection — the exact same race `mockup-engagement.spec.ts` already had to work around with an explicit wait, just never applied here. Fixed identically (`await expect(page.getByRole("combobox").first()).toContainText(...)` before filling the rest of the form); confirmed with 4 consecutive clean passes, previously flaky (roughly 1-in-3 failure rate in back-to-back isolated reruns).

**Not a bug**: `workflow-bypass-attempts.spec.ts` failed on a full-suite rerun against data that had already been seeded and then partially consumed by an *earlier* run of the same spec in this same session (it archives and recreates a specific seeded requirement as part of its own scenario, so it isn't idempotent against a stack it has already run against once) — confirmed by re-seeding the E2E dataset fresh (`scripts/seed_e2e_dataset.py` is itself idempotent-guarded and refuses to reseed over existing data, so this needed an explicit `TRUNCATE` first) and re-running the full suite once, cleanly. Consistent with this directory's own documented convention ("run the seed script once against a fresh stack before running this directory's specs") — not a regression, and not changed.

**Verified for real**: 3 new backend tests (52 total across the two most relevant files, all passing) plus the full backend suite — 322 passed, zero regressions. `tsc --noEmit`/`eslint` clean on every touched file. Both containers rebuilt; full Playwright suite — 23/23 passed clean on a freshly re-seeded stack, including both newly-fixed specs (`mockup-engagement.spec.ts`, `golden-path.spec.ts`) each independently reconfirmed with 4 consecutive isolated passes beforehand.

## Deeper hardening pass: three real gaps below the endpoint-level review

Requested follow-up to the previous entry ("go deeper"): the prior pass reviewed the endpoints this feature set added directly; this pass instead traced *invariants* across the whole login/provisioning surface those endpoints depend on — what happens to a role grant committed for an account that turns out to be deactivated, what "banned" is actually supposed to guarantee end-to-end, and whether org-level tenant isolation is the right granularity everywhere a new attachment reference was introduced. All three findings below were reachable pre-existing gaps that this feature set's own new code interacts with (two predate it entirely), not regressions introduced by the prior two rounds.

**Found and fixed: the OIDC login callback never checked account status at all.** `NativeAuthBackend.authenticate` rejects a deactivated, archived, or banned (`is_banned` implies `is_active=False`) account — `oidc_callback` (`routers/auth_oidc.py`) had no equivalent check anywhere in the flow. `get_current_user`'s own `is_active` check happens to make the resulting token inert against every other API call, but that's an incidental backstop, not a real control: before this fix, `consume_pending_invites` and `sync_org_roles_from_claims` still ran and *committed* brand-new org/project role grants for a deactivated or banned account during the rejected-in-effect login — grants that would then sit there silently until the account was later reactivated for an unrelated reason, at which point they'd become live without whoever reactivated it ever knowing new access had been granted while it was supposed to be locked down. Fixed by checking `is_active`/`is_archived` immediately after `find_or_provision_user` resolves the account and before either of those calls run, mirroring native login's check and its "specific, not generic, rejection message" reasoning (the caller already proved control of a real IdP-verified identity, so a distinct "This account has been deactivated" message leaks nothing enumerable). New tests drive the real HTTP callback end-to-end with `oidc_client.discover`/`exchange_code_for_tokens`/`verify_id_token` stubbed via `monkeypatch` (not just the `find_or_provision_user` building block, since the fix lives in the router between that call and the ones it gates): `test_oidc_callback_rejects_a_deactivated_account`, and `test_oidc_callback_rejects_a_banned_account_and_grants_no_pending_invite` — the latter specifically proves a `PendingInvite` outstanding for the email at the time of the attempt is left unconsumed, the concrete exploit this closes.

**Found and fixed: `reactivate_orphaned_user` let a banned account back in without ever going through `unban`.** `User.is_banned`'s own docstring states the invariant plainly: a ban "survives even if something else were to flip `is_active` back on" — but `reactivate_orphaned_user` (`routers/system.py`) only ever checked `_require_orphaned_user` (org membership) before unconditionally setting `is_active = True`, never `is_banned`. `unban_orphaned_user` deliberately does *not* restore `is_active` (its own test, `test_unban_allows_role_grants_again_but_does_not_reactivate`, pins exactly that), which only holds together as a real two-step control if `reactivate` in turn refuses a still-banned account — it didn't, so a single `/reactivate` call (no `/unban` needed at all) fully restored native login for a banned account, silently defeating the entire ban feature's stated purpose ("closing the gap ... where the same account could quietly be re-admitted ... without a server admin ever being asked again" — `ban_orphaned_user`'s own docstring). This was untested: no existing test called `/reactivate` on a still-banned account. Fixed by rejecting with 400 ("This account is banned. Unban it before reactivating.") when `user.is_banned`. New test: `test_reactivate_refuses_a_still_banned_account`, which also confirms the correct unban-then-reactivate sequence still works.

**Found and fixed: report image attachments were scoped to the wrong tenancy boundary.** `_resolve_report_images` (`routers/reports.py`) checked a referenced `attachment:<id>` against the report's `organization_id` — correct as far as it went (the previous round's own cross-org test already covers that), but org membership isn't the actual access boundary for every `FileAsset` in this codebase: `routers/files.py::download_file` gates a *direct* (non-shared, `is_org_resource=False`) attachment — most importantly a requirement attachment — on *project*-level access via `get_effective_project_roles`, specifically because a multi-project org's projects aren't mutually visible to each other's members. Report content has no such per-project scoping check of its own, so a user with report-edit rights on Project A could hand-type `attachment:<id>` referencing a requirement attachment that actually belongs to Project B in the same org — one they might have no project-level access to at all — and have its bytes embedded into Project A's generated report, silently bypassing `download_file`'s own finer-grained gate. Fixed by restricting resolution to `is_org_resource=True` assets only, which also matches what the feature's own UI actually offers: the "Insert image" picker (per the original plan and `RichTextEditor`) only ever lists org shared resources, never a raw requirement-attachment id, so this closes a hand-craftable gap without narrowing the real feature at all. New test: `test_report_pdf_silently_skips_a_same_org_requirement_attachment_from_a_different_project`.

**Reviewed and found adequate, no changes needed**: `assign_project_role_by_email`'s and `assign_project_role`'s lack of an `is_banned` check on the "target already an org member" path — traced through and confirmed unreachable in practice, since `ban_orphaned_user` only ever bans a genuinely orphaned (zero org roles) account and `assign_org_role` already refuses any *new* org-role grant to a banned account, so a banned user can never actually reach "already a member of this org" while still banned (the OIDC fix above closes the one path that could have gotten them there indirectly, via a pending invite consumed on login). `SignupRequest`'s password policy (`min_length=8`) matches the codebase's existing convention elsewhere, not a new relaxation.

**Verified for real**: 4 new backend tests (326 total, zero regressions) — the full suite, not just the new/changed files, since two of these fixes touch shared login/provisioning code paths (`test_oidc_provisioning.py`, `test_access_review.py`, `test_report_images.py`). Backend container rebuilt before every test run (this stack doesn't bind-mount source). Live dev/seed data was wiped by the pytest run against the shared `tests/container` database (the same known collision noted in the previous round) and restored via a backend restart + both seed scripts afterward.

## PDF reports restructured: chapter per component, sub-section per category

Request: PDF requirement tables should read as chapters per component with category sub-sections underneath, and Status should be the rightmost table column. Two design points were confirmed with the user before implementing: Component/Category columns are dropped from the per-category tables (redundant once expressed as chapter/section headings — table becomes ID, Name, Reasoning, Status), and each component chapter starts on a fresh page.

`generate_pdf_report` (`services/reports.py`) previously rendered one flat table of every requirement. It now groups `rows` via a new pure helper, `_group_rows_by_component_and_category`, pulled out specifically so the grouping/ordering logic is unit-testable without a PDF-text-extraction dependency (this project's test suite deliberately doesn't have one — see `test_report_images.py`). Ordering uses `ProjectComponent.sort_order`/`ProjectCategory.sort_order` — the same order the component/category tree UI uses — not alphabetical or requirement-ID order, since a report should read in the order an author actually arranged their tree, not incidentally by prefix. `ReportRequirementRow` gained `component_sort_order`/`category_sort_order` fields (populated in `routers/reports.py::_collect_rows` from the same `components`/`categories` lookups it already builds) to carry this through without a second DB round-trip. Grouped on `(sort_order, name)` rather than name alone, since component/category name uniqueness is only enforced per-parent (`(project_id, prefix)`/`(component_id, prefix)`), not project-wide — two same-named components at different tree positions must stay two distinct chapters, not silently merge.

The CSV export (`generate_csv_report`) is unchanged by request — it stays a flat, unique-code-sorted list with its own Component/Category columns, since it's for spreadsheet review, not read top-to-bottom as a document; `_collect_rows`'s own row order (still unique_code-sorted) is what CSV uses, only the PDF path re-groups.

Verified visually, not just via unit tests: generated a real PDF against the demo dataset's multi-component, multi-category "Falcon-3 Inspection Drone" project and rendered pages to PNG (`mcp__pdf-mcp__pdf_render_pages`) to confirm the actual visual column order — text extracted from a ReportLab table's cells doesn't reliably preserve left-to-right reading order, so this wasn't safe to assume from `pdf_read_all` output alone. Confirmed: chapter headings per component (each on a new page), sub-section headings per category, and the table reading ID / Name / Reasoning / **Status** left to right.

**Verified for real**: 4 new backend tests (3 unit tests directly on `_group_rows_by_component_and_category` covering sort-order-not-name ordering, within-category unique_code ordering, and same-named-different-position components staying separate; 1 router-level smoke test across multiple components/categories) plus the full backend suite — 330 passed, zero regressions. `ruff check .` clean. Visual PDF confirmation as described above.

## Report generation page: intro/template defaults, continuous-layout toggle, editors moved on-page

Follow-up request refining the chapter-per-component work above: the project description should default the PDF introduction; a project's selected default template should be pre-used on the report generation page; that page should show a template's content when selected, including body chapters, with the WYSIWYG/Markdown editors actually on the page (collapsed by default); Generate PDF/Generate CSV (renamed from Download PDF/Download CSV) should sit at the top; and chapter-per-component should be a toggle — on the template and the report page — defaulting to continuous only when no template is set *and* some component has fewer than three requirements (template or explicit user choice always wins).

**Schema** (new migration `0003_report_chapters_and_default_template.py`, same `IF NOT EXISTS` convention as `0002` for the same reason): `ReportTemplate.chapters_per_component` (`bool`, default `True`) and `Project.default_report_template_id` (nullable FK to `report_templates.id`, `ON DELETE SET NULL` — deleting a template must not break the project row, just clear the default).

**Intro fallback** (`resolve_report_config`): `project.report_intro` (explicit) → `project.summary` (the project description) → `org.default_report_intro` → blank. Description-as-intro is intro-only; there's no project-level equivalent to fall back to for chapters/appendices, so those keep their existing two-tier (project → org) resolution.

**Chapter-layout precedence** (`routers/reports.py::generate_pdf`, mirroring the doc comment on `ReportRequest.chapters_per_component`): an explicit per-generation choice (`ReportRequest.chapters_per_component: bool | None`) always wins; failing that, the selected template's own `chapters_per_component`; failing that (no template), a new `services.reports.default_chapters_per_component(rows)` heuristic — chaptered unless some component in the report's actual scope (post-filter) has fewer than three requirements, computed by re-using `_group_rows_by_component_and_category` rather than a second grouping pass. `generate_pdf_report` takes the resolved boolean directly; when `False`, the per-category headings/tables are unchanged, only the component-level heading and page break are skipped (confirmed with the user up front — "continuous" keeps category structure, it doesn't revert to the old single flat table).

**Default template**: `default_report_template_id` lives on `ProjectReportConfig` (GET/PUT `/projects/{id}/report-config`), not the general project endpoint — matches where `report_intro`/`chapters`/`appendices` already live, and where Project Admin's "Report Setup" tab already edits them. The PUT validates the template belongs to the project's own organisation (same check `generate_pdf` already applies to an ad-hoc `report_template_id`), otherwise a cross-org template could silently attach itself as a project's default. This is a pure UI default, not a backend fallback-when-null: the frontend pre-selects it in the report page's own dropdown, which the user can still change or clear per generation — deliberately not reinterpreting `report_template_id: null` as "use the project default" at the API layer, since that would remove the only way to explicitly request no template once a default exists.

**Report generation page rewrite** (`ReportsPage.tsx`): now fetches the project's effective report config (`GET /report-config`) alongside components/categories/resources/templates, and hosts the same `RichTextEditor`/`ReportChapterListEditor` components Project Admin's Report Setup tab already uses — but ad hoc: editing here only affects the report about to be generated (composed into `pre_markdown`/`post_markdown` client-side via a `chaptersToMarkdown` helper mirroring `_chapters_markdown` server-side), never written back to the project's saved config. Selecting a template recomputes the editors' content via the same per-field precedence `resolve_report_config_with_template` applies server-side (template's own field if set, else the project's effective content) — computed client-side against data already on the page rather than adding a preview endpoint. Every section (`Template & layout`, `Filters`, `Introduction`, `Body chapters`, `Appendices`, `Resource sections`) is a `CollapsibleSection` defaulting to collapsed, with `Generate PDF`/`Generate CSV` (renamed, matching the pattern this project's own generation semantics increasingly outgrew "Download" for) as the only always-visible controls, at the top.

Org Admin's template editor gained a `chapters_per_component` checkbox alongside the existing intro/chapters/appendices/branding fields; Project Admin's Report Setup tab gained the default-template picker.

**Verified for real**: 5 new backend tests (project-description intro fallback with explicit-intro-still-wins and both-blank-falls-to-org-default cases; the heuristic directly; router-level precedence proving payload beats template beats heuristic via a comparative PDF byte-size check, since a page break + heading is reliably more bytes than without; template `chapters_per_component` and project `default_report_template_id` round-trips including cross-org rejection) plus the full backend suite — 335 passed, zero regressions. `ruff check .` clean, `tsc -b && vite build` and `eslint .` clean on every changed frontend file. Verified live, not just via the test suite: generated a PDF against a project with only a description set and confirmed the description became the intro (`pdf_read_all`); generated a 1-page PDF with `chapters_per_component: false` against a 2-requirement component and rendered it to confirm the category heading/table appear with no component heading or page break (`pdf_render_pages`); drove the actual `ReportsPage` through a real browser session (Playwright MCP, per this project's "reserve for targeted one-off checks" rule) to confirm Generate PDF/CSV sit at the top, every section renders collapsed, and expanding Introduction shows the project's description already loaded into a full `RichTextEditor` (Markdown/Rich text toggle, Insert image). Full Playwright suite — 23/23 passed, including the two specs updated for the button rename (`golden-path.spec.ts`, `report-image-attachment.spec.ts`).

## Mandatory requirement target, optional description, change-request field-level tracking, comment attachments, and a locked-requirement attachment gate

An 8-item batch request: an "Add requirement" button on the Project Overview page; a "Make change request" button on the requirement detail page; change requests should only show/collect the fields they actually propose to change, pre-filled from the requirement so nothing needs re-typing; a requirement's target version can no longer be left unset; requirements gain an optional free-text description (also changeable via a change request); stakeholder vote comments should be viewable in a pop-up; the requirement change log should show who made each change; and direct requirement attachment should be blocked outside of creation/change-request approval, with attachment capability added to comments instead.

Four clarifying decisions were made with the user up front, all adopted as designed:

- **`RequirementLevel` gains a third member, `"optional"`**, rather than renaming `"recommended"` — the two are kept as distinct, coexisting levels.
- **Existing rows with a null `target_stage_id` are backfilled to each project's own earliest stage** (by `sort_order`) rather than an arbitrary "TBD" stage or a required manual migration step.
- **Change-request-proposed attachments only actually attach to the requirement once the change request is approved**, not immediately on draft — mirrors how every other proposed field already only applies on approval, so a withdrawn/rejected CR never leaves an orphaned attachment link behind.
- **The backend tracks which fields a change request actually changed** (`ChangeRequestVersion.changed_fields: list[str]`, one of `name, reasoning, clarification, description, target_stage_id, level, review_date, review_lead_days, reviewer_id, custom_fields, attachments`) rather than leaving "which fields to show/apply" as a frontend-only convenience. `POST /change-requests` validates `changed_fields` is non-empty for a MODIFY_REQUIREMENT CR and requires a non-null `proposed_*` value for every field listed that can't itself be null; on approval, `apply_new_version` is only passed a field's proposed value when that field is in `changed_fields` — everything else carries the current version's value forward unchanged, using the same `*_explicitly_set` carry-forward convention `target_stage_id`/`review_date`/`review_lead_days`/`reviewer_id` already used pre-Pelion, now extended uniformly to every proposable field instead of staying a special case for those four. This is the single source of truth the frontend's per-field checkbox UI reads and writes — there is no separate frontend-only diffing logic.

**`target_stage_id` becomes `NOT NULL`, and its FK becomes `ON DELETE CASCADE`.** Migration `0004` backfills nulls (correlated subquery: each requirement's own project's earliest stage) before setting the column `NOT NULL`. The FK's `ON DELETE` action had to change from `SET NULL` to `CASCADE` in the same migration — `SET NULL` against a now-`NOT NULL` column raises `IntegrityError` the instant a `ProjectStage` is ever deleted, which happens today only as a side effect of its whole project/organisation being deleted (there is no standalone `DELETE /stages/{id}` endpoint) — at which point every `RequirementVersion` referencing that stage is being deleted in the very same cascade anyway, so `CASCADE` here is a no-op in practice, not a real data-loss risk. Found via `test_org_lifecycle.py`'s existing cascade-delete test, which started raising `IntegrityError` the moment the column went `NOT NULL` — a genuine bug this change would otherwise have shipped, not a pre-existing gap.

**Comment attachments are the one place a user may still attach a file to a requirement outside of creation or an approved change request.** A new `comment_files` join table (`ReviewComment` ↔ `FileAsset`) backs `POST /comments/{id}/files` on both the requirement and change-request routers (mirrored, matching this codebase's existing precedent of per-router-duplicated comment endpoints over the shared `ReviewComment` model). `routers/requirements.py`'s direct-attachment endpoints (`upload_requirement_attachment`, `link_org_resource`) now 409 once the requirement is locked; comment attachments are deliberately exempt from that check, since a discussion thread isn't C-G-12-governed content. This also surfaced a real, pre-existing access-control gap in `routers/files.py::download_file`: the generic file-download endpoint had no resolution path at all for a comment-attached file, so it would 403 *everyone*, including the uploader — fixed by extending its access resolution to walk `CommentFile → ReviewComment → (Requirement | ChangeRequest)` for the owning project, the same pattern already used for `RequirementFile`-linked downloads.

**Frontend**: `ChangeRequestsPage.tsx`'s create form now shows, for a MODIFY_REQUIREMENT change request, a checkbox per changeable field (label set: Name, Reasoning, Clarification, Description, Target version, Level, Review date, Reminder lead time, Assigned reviewer, Custom fields, Attachments) — checking one reveals an editor pre-filled from the selected requirement's current value, and only checked fields are submitted in `changed_fields`/`proposed_*`. A NEW_REQUIREMENT CR keeps a normal always-visible create form, since there's no existing requirement to diff against. `ChangeRequestDetailPage.tsx` mirrors this on display: a MODIFY_REQUIREMENT CR only renders the fields actually in `changed_fields`, instead of always showing every proposed value regardless of whether it was touched. Attachments reuse the existing org-shared-resource picker/upload flow (same mechanism the report-image "Insert image" picker already established) rather than a new upload endpoint. A new reusable `Modal.tsx` component (portal-rendered, Escape/backdrop-dismissible) backs the stakeholder-vote-comments pop-up on `ChangeRequestDetailPage.tsx` — the vote data (`ChangeRequestVoteTallyOut.votes`) already carried every voter's comment, so this was purely a rendering gap, not a backend change. The requirement change log gained a "Changed by" column from `RequirementVersionEntry.created_by`, which was likewise already returned by the API and only needed surfacing.

Two real frontend bugs were found via an actual browser session (not just `tsc`/`eslint`/unit tests, which stayed clean throughout) while verifying the "Make change request" deep link (`/change-requests?requirement=<id>`, opened from the requirement detail page) pre-selects the right requirement:

- **Stale-closure race clobbering the deep-linked selection.** `ChangeRequestsPage`'s data-loading `reload()` defaults `requirementId` to the project's first requirement when none is set yet; a separate effect sets `requirementId` to the deep-linked id and then calls `setSearchParams` to strip the `?requirement=` param. `setSearchParams`'s updater mutates the underlying `URLSearchParams` instance **in place** rather than returning a copy — so by the time `reload()`'s own in-flight `await` resolved, its closure's live reference to that same mutable object read back `null` for the just-stripped param, even though the value had been correct at the point `reload()` started. Fixed by capturing the param's value into a local `const` synchronously, before the first `await`, rather than re-reading the (shared, mutable) `searchParams` object after it.
- **Prefill effect never firing when the requirement list hadn't loaded yet.** The effect that pre-fills the "Fields to change" editors from the selected requirement's current version depended only on `[requirementId, kind]`; when the deep link set `requirementId` before the requirements list itself had finished loading, `selectedRequirement` was `null` on that pass, the effect bailed out, and — since `requirements` finishing loading later wasn't in its dependency array — it never re-ran. Fixed by adding `requirements` to the dependency array so the effect re-evaluates once the list the deep-linked id needs to resolve against actually arrives.

**Verified for real**: full backend suite (348 tests, including a new `test_change_request_field_tracking.py` covering target-defaulting, `changed_fields` validation, unlisted-field-never-applied-on-approval, locked-requirement attachment rejection, change-request attachment only applying on approval, cross-org attachment rejection, and comment-attachment upload/download/cross-project isolation) passing, `ruff check .` clean. `tsc --noEmit`, `eslint`, and `vite build` clean across every changed frontend file. Live-browser verification (Playwright MCP) of the full loop: created a requirement with the new mandatory pre-filled target and new description field; opened it and used "Make change request" to deep-link into a correctly-pre-selected, correctly-pre-filled MODIFY_REQUIREMENT form; toggled only the Description field, submitted, submitted-for-review, cast an advisory vote with a comment, opened it via the new "View comments" pop-up, approved; confirmed the approved requirement's changelog showed both the change and the correct "Changed by" actor, and that its Attachments section now shows the locked notice instead of an upload control.

## Comment editing/attachment redesign, renaming and deleting project stages/components/categories, and two notification bugs

Three more items from the same feedback batch: comment file attachments were reported as "the button doesn't work" (see below for the real bug) and should only ever be added while composing or editing your own comment (not as a persistent post-hoc action on any comment); anyone who can create/delete project stages/components/categories should also be able to rename them, and deleting one should require reassigning whatever it currently governs to another existing one rather than being blocked outright — except when that would be genuinely unsafe; and the notification list/dropdown had unreadable text plus no click-through to the item that triggered it.

Two decisions were confirmed with the user before implementing the structural-deletion piece, since it directly intersects data this codebase already treats as immutable history:

- **Deleting a stage that has an approved baseline is refused outright, not reassigned.** A `Baseline` is documented as an *immutable* snapshot of a specific stage's approved requirements (C-G-10) — silently repointing `Baseline.stage_id` at a different stage on deletion would quietly rewrite what that historical snapshot means. Every other stage reference (every `RequirementVersion.target_stage_id`, across *all* historical versions, and every `ChangeRequestVersion.proposed_target_stage_id`) is reassigned to `reassign_to`; only baselines block the delete. Renaming a stage remains available regardless, and archiving is unaffected — this only closes off outright deletion of a stage with real approval history.
- **Comments may only be edited by their own author** — not by a project manager/administrator either. A PM correcting or moderating someone else's words was considered and explicitly rejected in favour of the simpler, more conservative rule: nobody edits content they didn't write. The same author-only rule was extended to attaching/removing a comment's files, since those are really just another part of "editing your own comment."

**Comment editing + attachment redesign** (`backend/app/models/change_request.py::ReviewComment`, `backend/app/schemas/requirement.py`, `backend/app/routers/requirements.py`, `backend/app/routers/change_requests.py`, migration `0005_comment_editing.py`):
- `ReviewComment.edited_at: datetime | None`, exposed as `CommentOut.edited_at` — null until an edit happens. Deliberately its own column rather than comparing `created_at`/`updated_at`: `TimestampMixin`'s two independent `default=utcnow` callables can resolve a few microseconds apart on the very same INSERT, which would make a freshly-created, never-edited comment intermittently render as "(edited)".
- New `PATCH .../comments/{comment_id}` (both routers) — author-only, updates `body` and stamps `edited_at`.
- New `DELETE .../comments/{comment_id}/files/{file_id}` (both routers) — author-only; always deletes the underlying `FileAsset` outright (comment attachments are never `is_org_resource` shared links the way requirement attachments sometimes are, so there's no unlink-vs-delete branch to choose between, unlike `unlink_requirement_file`).
- **The actual bug behind "the button doesn't work"**: `POST .../comments/{comment_id}/files` had no author check at all — any project viewer could attach a file to *anyone's* comment, at any time, forever. That's not what "attach a file to your comment" should mean, and it's also inconsistent with there being no way to remove one. Tightened to author-only (403 otherwise) as part of this same change, alongside the new remove endpoint — together these make "attach" and "remove" symmetric, author-scoped actions rather than the previous asymmetric "anyone can add, no one can remove" state.
- **Frontend** (`CommentThread.tsx`, rewritten): the attach control now only appears in two places — the compose box for a *new* comment (staged locally as pending `File` objects, uploaded only after the comment itself is created, chained off the id the create call returns) and inline when editing your own existing comment (existing attachments shown with a remove button, new ones stageable the same way, both applied on Save). There is no more persistent per-comment attach button on an already-posted comment you're not editing. `onPost`'s return type changed from `Promise<void>` to `Promise<Comment>` specifically so `CommentThread` can chain the staged-file uploads off the newly-created comment's id — both `RequirementDetailPage.tsx` and `ChangeRequestDetailPage.tsx`'s `postComment` were updated to match. Comments show "(edited)" next to their timestamp once `edited_at` is set; the Edit control itself only renders when `currentUserId === comment.author_id`.

**Stage/component/category rename** (`routers/projects.py`): plain `PATCH` endpoints (`ProjectStageUpdate`/`ComponentUpdate`/`CategoryUpdate`), same `require_project_manage` gate as the existing create/move endpoints, with the same collision validation create already does (stage name unique per project, component prefix unique per project, category prefix unique per *component* — reusable across different components' categories, since `ProjectCategory`'s constraint is `(component_id, prefix)` not `(project_id, prefix)`). Renaming a component/category's prefix never rewrites already-generated `Requirement.unique_code` values (e.g. `SW-PERF-014` stays `SW-PERF-014`) — codes are assigned once at creation and are permanent identifiers, not a live label.

**Stage/component/category delete-with-reassignment**: all three share the shape "take `reassign_to`, refuse if it doesn't resolve to a different, existing sibling in the same project" — which turns out to make the "can't delete the last one" rule fall out for free rather than needing its own special-cased check: if there's only one stage/category left, no valid `reassign_to` can ever exist, so the existing 400 validation already covers it.
- **Stage** (`DELETE .../stages/{id}?reassign_to=`): reassigns every `RequirementVersion.target_stage_id` (all versions, not just current — `target_stage_id` is `NOT NULL`, so this can't be left dangling) and every `ChangeRequestVersion.proposed_target_stage_id` equal to the deleted stage; transfers `is_current` to `reassign_to` if the deleted stage held it, so a project is never left with zero current stages. Blocked (409) if any `Baseline` references the stage — see decision above.
- **Category** (`DELETE .../categories/{id}?reassign_to=`): `reassign_to` may be a category under a **different** component than the one being deleted (deliberately not restricted to siblings the way `move_category`'s *ordering* is) — reassigns every affected `Requirement.category_id` **and** `component_id` together, preserving the invariant (enforced at requirement-creation time) that a requirement's component always matches its category's parent component even when the reassignment crosses component boundaries.
- **Component** (`DELETE .../components/{id}`, no `reassign_to`): refused (409) unless the component already has zero categories. Since every requirement's `component_id` is enforced to match its category's component at creation, a component with zero categories necessarily also has zero requirements referencing it directly — so once its categories have each been deleted/reassigned (via the category endpoint above, which can target a category under any other component), the component itself becomes a plain, unconditional delete with nothing left to reassign.

**Notification fixes** (`frontend/src/components/NotificationBell.tsx`, `frontend/src/pages/NotificationsPage.tsx`, `frontend/src/styles/theme.css`, `frontend/src/api/types.ts`):
- **The actual "white on white" bug**: the notification bell's dropdown is a normal light `.card` popover, but it's rendered as a DOM descendant of `.app-header` (the bell icon lives in the header bar) — `.app-header` sets a header-tuned white `color` and overrides `.btn`/`.text-muted` for text sitting directly on its own dark bar, and the dropdown never reset any of that back to normal page colours despite visually being a light surface, not header chrome. Fixed with a `.notification-dropdown` class and three CSS rules (`.app-header .notification-dropdown` / `...text-muted` / `...btn`) specific enough to win over the header's own rules regardless of source order — confirmed visually via a screenshot, not just by reading the CSS, since a plain diff can't show what actually renders.
- **Click-through**: `notificationLink()` (`api/types.ts`) resolves a notification's `entity_type`/`entity_id`/`project_id` to a route (`requirement` → the requirement's page, `change_request` → the CR's page, `project_stage` → that project's admin page, otherwise falls back to the project overview if a `project_id` is present at all, else not navigable) — the same "resolve from what the activity feed already carries" approach `activityEntryLink` already used for the project-history feed, just applied to notifications for the first time. Clicking a notification now marks it read (as before) and navigates, in both the header dropdown and the full `/notifications` page.

**Verified for real**: full backend suite — 371 tests total (23 new: 17 stage/component/category rename+delete permission/validation/reassignment-correctness cases in a new `test_project_structure_admin.py`, plus 6 comment-editing/attachment-author-only cases added to `test_change_request_field_tracking.py`) — passing, `ruff check .` clean. `tsc --noEmit`, `eslint`, and `vite build` clean. Live-browser verification (Playwright MCP): posted a comment with a compose-time attachment, edited it (confirmed body change, attachment preservation, and the "(edited)" label all render correctly), renamed a stage then deleted it with reassignment (confirmed via the requirements list that every requirement previously targeting it now shows the reassignment target, with no leftover reference to the deleted stage anywhere), renamed then deleted a category with a *cross-component* reassignment target (confirmed the affected requirements' component/category badges updated to the new component, while their historical `unique_code` prefix stayed unchanged as designed), and confirmed the notification dropdown's text/buttons are legible (screenshot) and that clicking a change-request notification navigates straight to that change request.

## Container image publishing and version tagging

The `docker-build` CI job (see "Continuous integration" above) previously built and tagged the production images but deliberately never pushed them, per an earlier explicit instruction to hold off until the system had seen enough real-world usage to trust an automated publish. Per a later explicit instruction, that's now reversed: the job pushes `backend`, `frontend`, and `mcp-server` to `ghcr.io/<owner>/<repo>-{backend,frontend,mcp-server}` on every push to `main` — pull request runs still build all three (still proving the Dockerfiles work) but never push, since a same-repo PR's `GITHUB_TOKEN` could technically publish but a feature branch has no business overwriting `:latest` (or any tag) in the registry a real deployment pulls from. No new secret was needed: `GITHUB_TOKEN` already had `packages: write` at the job level from when the job was first written build-only.

**Version tagging via GitVersion.** Each image now gets three tags instead of two: the existing `<commit sha>` and `latest`, plus a SemVer computed by [GitVersion](https://gitversion.net/) (new `GitVersion.yml`, repo root) from the commit graph. The repo has no git tags and only one long-lived branch, so `mode: Mainline` was the only configuration that made sense here — every merge to `main` is treated as a release and the version increments automatically (starting at `0.1.0`), rather than requiring someone to push an annotated tag by hand for every release the way GitVersion's default `ContinuousDelivery` mode would. This gives deployments a stable, human-meaningful version to pin `IMAGE_TAG` to (see below) without adding any manual release-tagging step to the workflow. `actions/checkout` needed `fetch-depth: 0` added (full history, not the default single shallow commit) for GitVersion to have a commit graph to compute from at all.

**`docker-compose.yml` updated to match.** The root (production) compose file previously only had `build:` for `backend`/`frontend`/`mcp-server`, meaning a local Dockerfile build was the *only* way to run it — appropriate when nothing was ever published, wrong now that images exist. Each of the three now also declares `image: ${GHCR_IMAGE_PREFIX:-ghcr.io/ballardr/reqtrackmanager}-<service>:${IMAGE_TAG:-latest}` alongside the existing `build:`. Both were kept, deliberately, rather than replacing `build:` outright: `docker compose up --build -d` (or a plain `up`/`pull` before any image has ever been published) still works exactly as before with no registry involved, while `IMAGE_TAG=<semver-or-sha>` plus `docker compose pull && docker compose up -d` becomes the faster upgrade path — pull a specific released version and restart, no source checkout or local build needed on the deployment host at all. `GHCR_IMAGE_PREFIX` is separately overridable for anyone mirroring the images into a different registry/namespace.

Documented in `docs/deployment.md`'s new "Obtaining container images" section (tag meanings, the `IMAGE_TAG`/`GHCR_IMAGE_PREFIX` variables, the pull-instead-of-build upgrade path) and the README's "Continuous integration" section (which previously ended with "does not publish them yet — see the comments... for exactly what to uncomment when ready"; that instruction has now been followed).

**Verified**: `docker compose config` against the updated root `docker-compose.yml` (with the required secrets stubbed in) resolves cleanly and the three `image:` lines interpolate to the expected `ghcr.io/ballardr/reqtrackmanager-{backend,frontend,mcp-server}:latest` with no `IMAGE_TAG`/`GHCR_IMAGE_PREFIX` set, confirming the defaults are correct before any image has actually been pushed by the updated workflow.

## README restructure: product-facing content, dev workflow split out, nginx doubling investigated

The README had drifted into two audiences at once — a prospective user evaluating the product and a contributor setting up a dev environment — with the latter (full local backend/frontend/test/lint/CI setup) taking up roughly half the file. Per explicit instruction, restructured along that seam:

- **New `docs/development.md`**: the "Quick start — local development / evaluation" section (including Demo data) and the entire "Development workflow" section (backend/frontend setup, backend/E2E tests, linting, CI) moved there verbatim, since none of it is genuinely "what is this product" content. `CLAUDE.md`'s README Requirements section (which mandates a "Development workflow" section in the README) was updated to make explicit that a short section linking to `docs/development.md` satisfies this, rather than requiring the full instructions duplicated inline — otherwise this move would have contradicted the project's own checked-in documentation policy.
- **README rewritten around product content**: the intro was expanded from a single dense paragraph into three sections — the requirements workflow (authoring, versioning, stage baselining, change requests with tasks/voting, discussion threads, review scheduling), team-workflow features (RBAC, custom fields/terminology/templates, notifications), and an enterprise-features section (SSO/OIDC, PATs scoped per org/project, application-layer encryption at rest, the MCP server, observability, the SOC 2 policy package, and the SCIM/provisioning blueprint) — covering exactly the three areas asked for (requirements, workflows, enterprise features) without inventing capabilities the codebase doesn't have; every claim traces to an existing feature already documented elsewhere (`docs/requirements.md`, `docs/enterprise-integration.md`, `docs/mcp-server.md`).
- **`CLAUDE.md` documentation-requirements exception**: added an explicit carve-out that README screenshots don't need "what it shows / how to read it / why it matters" explanatory prose the way Mermaid diagrams do — a short caption is enough. Written to stop a future documentation pass from padding the screenshots table to satisfy the diagram rule, which was never the rule's intent.
- **Screenshots recaptured** (all 9, at the same 1440×900 viewport as before) against the live dev/test stack with `scripts/seed_demo_data.py`'s dataset — the existing ones predated the recent white-logo header change and a couple of UI states shown (e.g. an org-admin screenshot with a stray autofilled password visible in the "new user" form) weren't clean. Logged in as `demo.admin@example.com` and re-walked every page the original set covered; the org-admin capture additionally has its scratch "new user" form fields cleared first so nothing that looks like a credential appears in a published screenshot, even though the underlying values were fake bootstrap-only demo credentials.
- **Production deployment section expanded in the README itself** (not just deployment.md) per instruction to actually *discuss* what's needed rather than link past it: a concrete minimal `.env`, and a paragraph each on the three "extra" components — MinIO (default S3-compatible storage, swappable for real S3), per-organisation OIDC/SSO (no bundled IdP in production; Keycloak is dev/test-only), and the MCP server (optional, credential-less, forwards the caller's own token) — plus both reverse-proxy patterns with a working nginx snippet for the same-origin one.

**The `/api/api/v1` doubling investigation** (prompted by "I think the current nginx example would end up with the backend on `/api/api/v1`"): tested for real against the live dev/test stack rather than reasoned about from the config alone. Ran a throwaway nginx container on the same Docker network implementing `docs/deployment.md`'s exact same-origin subpath config (`location /api/ { proxy_pass http://backend:8000; }`), then compared `GET /api/v1/system/branding` through the proxy against the same request straight to the backend: identical status (`401`) and identical body, and the extra one-to-one `/api/docs`/`/api/openapi.json` blocks likewise matched their direct equivalents (`200`, byte-identical `openapi.json`). **The nginx config itself is correct** — `proxy_pass http://backend:8000;` with no URI component forwards the original request path unchanged, and that path already carries `/api/v1/...` from the frontend's own hardcoded route literals, so there is no doubling anywhere in the documented example.

The real doubling risk is a `PUBLIC_API_BASE_URL` misconfiguration, not the proxy: the frontend always prepends `/api/v1/...` (baked into its own request-building code, `frontend/src/api/client.ts`) onto whatever `PUBLIC_API_BASE_URL` resolves to, so the intuitive-but-wrong choice of `PUBLIC_API_BASE_URL=/api` (matching the proxy's own location prefix, which feels like it should be "the API's base URL") produces requests to `/api` + `/api/v1/...` = `/api/api/v1/...`, which 404s. Confirmed this directly too — ran the actual built frontend image with `VITE_API_BASE_URL=/api` and inspected its generated `env-config.js`, showing the literal wrong value that would be prepended. Deliberately not "fixed" in code: the doc already specified `PUBLIC_API_BASE_URL=` (empty) as correct, and adding logic to strip a redundant `/api` prefix would paper over a config mistake in a way that could silently break a deployment whose API genuinely lives under a different `/api`-containing path for unrelated reasons. Instead, `docs/deployment.md`'s same-origin subpath section gained an explicit "Common mistake" callout naming the wrong value, explaining the mechanism, and cross-referencing this verification, plus a note on the existing "verified against a real reverse proxy" line recording the re-verification and its scope.

**Verified**: all of the above — the proxy/doubling comparisons were live `curl` diffs against real containers (not just described), not synthetic. `tsc`/build not re-run for this pass (no frontend/backend code changed, only Markdown and the two throwaway nginx/frontend test containers, both torn down afterward).

## CI failure: three Playwright specs broken by the component/category rename UI and the comment-attachment redesign

CI failed on the merge of the batch above (`docker-build` never ran; `backend-and-e2e` failed with 3/23 Playwright specs red: `golden-path.spec.ts`, `mockup-engagement.spec.ts`, `pelion-v2.spec.ts`, all in the same "add component and category" step). Diagnosed against the real dev/test stack rather than guessed from the stack trace, since the failure mode (a button staying disabled after filling its inputs) had more than one plausible cause.

**Root cause #1 — stale selector, not a real bug.** All three specs asserted `getByText("Software")`/`getByText("Performance")` etc. right after creating a component/category, from before component/category names were rename-able. The rename feature (this same batch, see "Comment editing/attachment redesign, renaming and deleting..." above) changed those names from static text to always-editable `<input value={...}>` fields with no `placeholder`/label — so the name only ever exists as an input's *value*, never as a text node, and `getByText` correctly found nothing. Playwright has no `getByDisplayValue` (that's a Testing Library API, not Playwright's — confirmed by trying it: `TypeError: page.getByDisplayValue is not a function`); fixed with `page.locator('input[value="X"]')`, first verifying live that React does mirror the `value` prop onto the DOM attribute for these inputs (checked via `element.getAttribute('value')` against a real running page), so the CSS attribute selector is reliable here.

**Root cause #2 — a real, if narrow, app-level race, not just a test bug.** Fixing root cause #1 got further but still failed: the "New category" button stayed disabled even after filling its Name/Prefix fields immediately following component creation. Root-caused with a `MutationObserver` attached to the real running app (not guessed): `ProjectAdminPage.tsx`'s `reload()` (called after every mutation) fires 7 requests concurrently via `Promise.all`, then two *more* awaited **sequentially** afterwards (`setOrgUsers`, `setReportTemplates`) — unrelated to the Categories tab, but still state on the same component, so each still triggers a re-render. That second wave lands ~150-300ms after the new component itself first becomes visible, which is enough to reset whatever's mid-typed into its own newly-rendered "add category" form if a script (or a very fast user) starts typing immediately. Confirmed the exact timing via the observer before treating it as the explanation, not after.

Deliberately not "fixed" by making `reload()` a single atomic state update or debouncing it — that's a real app-level improvement worth doing at some point (two of `reload()`'s 9 requests are unrelated to most of what triggers it and don't need to block anything), but out of scope for a CI-red fix and risked introducing a real regression under time pressure to chase a real one. Fixed at the test layer instead: `await page.waitForLoadState("networkidle")` after the component becomes visible, before touching its form, rides past the whole settling window regardless of exactly how many re-renders cause it. Also scoped the subsequent `getByPlaceholder("Name"/"Prefix")` calls to the specific new component's own container (`input[value="X"].locator("xpath=../../..")`, three levels up per the actual JSX nesting) rather than a page-wide `.first()`, since with rename inputs now also using those placeholders, "first Name/Prefix pair on the page" is no longer an assumption safe to make.

**A third, unrelated latent bug found while fixing `pelion-v2.spec.ts`**: after both fixes above, that spec progressed further and failed at a completely different step — `input[type="file"]` resolved to 2 elements instead of 1. The comment-attachment redesign (same batch) added an always-present file input to the comment compose box (`CommentThread.tsx`), which now collides with the requirement's own "Attachments" card file input on the same page — a selector that was unambiguous before this batch. Fixed by scoping to the "Attachments" card specifically (`page.locator(".card", { has: page.getByRole("heading", { name: "Attachments" }) })`) rather than a page-wide file-input query. Checked the rest of the suite for the same pattern (`grep` across `tests/playwright/tests/`) — only this one site was affected; `report-image-attachment.spec.ts`'s own file-input query already scoped via `[accept="image/*"]`, which doesn't collide.

## Full-product QA pass: 15 new Playwright specs closing every remaining page/workflow gap, three real bugs found and fixed, ten cross-spec test-isolation races found and fixed

Asked to act as QA lead for a team new to the project: review the whole product UI end-to-end against `docs/requirements.md` and this log, and add Playwright coverage so every page and workflow is tested, with business logic verified as backend-enforced rather than just hidden client-side. Full detail (personas, workflow catalogue, every gap found) lives in `docs/e2e-workflows.md`, which is this suite's own living document; this entry records the decisions and the real bugs, per this log's usual scope.

**Gap inventory.** Cross-checked the full route list (`frontend/src/App.tsx`, 23 pages) against `docs/requirements.md` and this log's implementation history. Massif (v3) is fully built except SCIM/separate-port provisioning (out of scope per the existing decision above); several post-Massif rounds added self-signup/invites, report images, and CR field-tracking. Fourteen genuinely untested areas remained — entire pages or backend-enforced rules with zero Playwright coverage: review scheduling, change-request tasks/voting, stage review deadlines/completion, the structural/groups/fields/templates halves of Project and Org Admin, org-wide 2FA, the server-admin user directory and bans, notifications/favourites, project history, comment editing/attachments, the CSV import wizard, requirement/CR filters, and preferences/theme/landing-page. Closed with 15 new spec files under `tests/playwright/tests/e2e-workflows/`, one per cohesive area, following this suite's existing convention (real UI interaction paired with a direct API call proving server-side enforcement wherever a permission or workflow-state rule is involved). Two personas (`stakeholderAlpha2`, `orphan`) and their helpers were added to `seed_e2e_dataset.py`/`helpers.ts` alongside the existing five, idempotent-guarded the same way the script already was. AI-assisted requirement writing, IBM DOORS import, SCIM, and the separate provisioning port were skipped silently, per standing scope decisions above.

**Three real product bugs, found live and fixed** (identify → verify → remediate, since two touch workflow-state enforcement):
- **No UI path existed to put a project stage into `review` status at all.** `ProjectAdminPage.tsx`'s only stage action jumped `scoping → approved` directly; the review-deadline/stakeholder-response UI (C-R-05) only ever renders once a stage is *in* review, which nothing in the product could reach — flagged to the user before fixing, per this project's standing "query unexpected behaviour rather than silently work around it" instruction. Fixed with a new "Start review" action, plus real state-machine validation added to `POST /stages/{id}/transition` (`_ALLOWED_STAGE_TRANSITIONS` in `routers/projects.py`): a stage can no longer skip a step (`scoping → approved`) or move backwards (`approved → scoping`), which would otherwise silently unlock already-locked requirements outside the change-request process; `COMPLETED` stays reachable only via the dedicated `/complete` endpoint. New backend test `test_stage_transition_rejects_skipping_and_backwards_moves`; four pre-existing backend tests updated to go through `review` first, and `seed_demo_data.py` (missed in the same pass, caught later when a reseed broke — see below) updated the same way.
- **`GET /projects/{id}/report-config` was gated to manager-only, silently breaking the Reports and Project Admin pages for stakeholders/members.** Same failure class as the previously-documented "OrgAdminPage hangs forever" bug (see "UI/UX audit" above): one over-privileged fetch inside a page's single `Promise.all` reload fails the whole page load. C-U-03 lets stakeholders/members generate reports, so `ReportsPage.tsx` was already silently 403ing there too, independent of the admin-page symptom that surfaced it. Fixed by loosening the GET to `require_project_view` (the PUT stays manager-only); new test `test_report_config_is_readable_by_a_plain_stakeholder_not_just_a_manager`.
- **`ToggleSwitch` didn't stop click propagation.** `CollapsibleSection` wraps its title in a clickable header; the 2FA-enrollment toggle is the one place a `ToggleSwitch` renders inside that title, so enabling it also bubbled up and immediately collapsed the section, hiding the QR/confirmation UI the toggle had just revealed. Fixed with `e.stopPropagation()` in `ToggleSwitch.tsx` itself, not just the one call site, since the same nesting could recur.

**Verification methodology, and a real lesson in it.** Each new spec was written and passed individually against its own fresh reseed. Running the full suite together (38 specs, `workers: 1`, sequential) then surfaced a *second* class of failure individual runs can't catch: ten specs (eight of mine, one pre-existing seed script, one pre-existing spec's undocumented dependency) whose passing depended on assumptions that only hold on a database only one spec has touched.
