None were product bugs; full root-cause detail for each (a stale hard-coded requirement name a different spec legitimately mutates, four `Locator.count()`-doesn't-auto-wait races, an unscoped placeholder query colliding with a same-page SMTP field, and this doc's own reseed recipe missing a required seed script) is in `docs/e2e-workflows.md`'s "Full-suite hardening" section rather than duplicated here. The methodology lesson is worth recording here though: the first attempt to *confirm* the fixes by re-running the full suite again without a fresh reseed in between produced 9 failures in specs that had never failed before — an artifact of two prior runs plus interactive MCP-browser debugging having left the shared database in states those specs never anticipated, not a regression. The only valid verification turned out to be a full clean-slate reseed (`down -v && up --build -d`, backend pytest as the truncate step, restart, both seed scripts) followed by exactly one suite run with no manual interference — confirmed clean (38/38) on the fourth attempt, after the first three each surfaced genuine issues to fix.

**Verified for real**: backend pytest — 373 passed — after each of two clean-slate reseeds in this pass (confirming the stage-transition change didn't regress anything, and again after fixing `seed_demo_data.py` against it). Full Playwright suite (38 specs, old + new) — 38 passed — against a genuinely fresh reseed with zero interference, the only run of the four attempts that counts as real confirmation per the methodology note above.

**Verified**: all three affected specs re-run individually until green, then the full 23-spec Playwright suite (`23 passed`) and the full backend suite (`371 passed`) both run clean against the real dev/test stack — not just the three fixed specs in isolation, catching any other fallout from the same rename/attachment UI changes. Per the stack's known pytest-truncates-the-schema behavior, restarted `backend` and reseeded both `seed_demo_data.py` and `seed_e2e_dataset.py` afterward before considering the stack verified-clean again.

## CI: GitVersion 6 removed Mainline mode (real run failure), frontend test/coverage badges added, and a badge-publishing race fixed

The `docker-build` job's real run on `main` failed at "Determine version" with `GitVersion output is not valid JSON` and no further detail in the Actions log — the underlying `dotnet-gitversion` process produced no output at all, which the wrapper action then couldn't parse. Diagnosed by running the actual `gittools/gitversion` tool locally (via its official Docker image) against both a throwaway repo and the real repo checked out to `main`, rather than guessing from the truncated Actions log: `mode: Mainline` — valid in GitVersion 5.x, and what `GitVersion.yml` was still set to — was removed entirely in GitVersion 6.0 (only `ContinuousDelivery`/`ContinuousDeployment` remain), so the config file itself was rejected before GitVersion could even attempt a version calculation. This had never actually been exercised: the prior "Container image publishing" pass (see above) only verified `docker compose config`'s tag *interpolation* against hypothetical values, never ran GitVersion itself for real.

**Fixed** by switching to `mode: ContinuousDeployment` (GitVersion 6's replacement for Mainline's "every commit on the tracked branch is a release" behaviour — unlike `ContinuousDelivery`, it produces a clean `X.Y.Z` with no prerelease suffix on every commit, which is what a container tag needs) plus an explicit `next-version: 0.1.0`, since GitVersion's own default next-version starts a repo with no tags at `0.0.1`, not the `0.1.0` the file's comment already (aspirationally, and incorrectly until now) claimed. Verified against the real tool both ways: a throwaway repo with a minimal `mode: Mainline` config reproduces the exact same "Invalid enum scalar 'Mainline'" exception GitVersion itself throws (the Actions wrapper just swallows it), and the actual repo checked out to `main` with the fixed config computes `SemVer: "0.1.0"` correctly.

**Frontend coverage/test badges** (asked for alongside the CI fix): the `frontend` job previously only linted, type-checked, and built — no test step existed in CI at all, even though `npm run test-storybook` (Storybook stories run as real-Chromium Vitest browser tests) already worked locally and is documented in `docs/development.md`. Added coverage collection to `vite.config.ts` (`test.coverage`, root-level rather than per-project since Vitest's workspace/"projects" mode doesn't allow per-project coverage config) with a `json-summary` reporter for badge parsing, `test.reporters: ["default", "junit"]` for a JUnit report mirroring the backend's `pytest-report.xml`, and a CI step running `npm run test-storybook -- --coverage` with the same coverage-summary/job-summary/artifact-upload treatment the backend already gets. Frontend coverage starts low (~0.2%, honestly reported, not massaged) since only two component stories exist today and most page-level logic is instead covered by the Playwright E2E suite, which this number doesn't measure — noted explicitly in both the job summary and `development.md` so a future reader doesn't mistake it for "untested."

**A real, Vitest-specific gotcha found via local `act` runs** (see the new "Testing the pipeline locally with `act`" section in `development.md`): `npm run test-storybook -- --coverage` reliably completed its actual work — every test ran, the coverage report and JUnit XML were both written correctly — and then never exited, hanging the step (and, unbounded, the whole job) indefinitely. Two `act`-specific causes were ruled out and fixed along the way (a plain `docker run` container's tiny default `/dev/shm` crashing headless Chromium — `--shm-size=2gb`; no init process to reap the crashed browser's zombie child — `--init`), neither of which affects real GitHub-hosted runners (full VMs, not nested containers) and so aren't part of `ci.yml` itself. But even with both fixed and the browser no longer crashing, the parent `vitest` process still didn't return control — a real `@vitest/browser` + Playwright-provider quirk, reproduced consistently, not an artifact of the container. Fixed at the CI-step level: wrapped the command in `timeout 180`, and on a nonzero exit, parse `vitest-report.xml` directly rather than trusting the process's own exit code — a real per-test failure (any `failures`/`errors` in the JUnit output) still fails the step, but a hang-after-success is caught and treated as the pass it actually was. Chose this over chasing further `act`/Vitest configuration, since the actual test/coverage output was already provably correct both times it was reproduced.

**Badge-publishing race, found while wiring the frontend badges up, not by observing a real failure yet.** The backend's existing badge-publish step force-pushes a fresh, from-scratch git history straight to the `badges` branch from *inside* the `backend-and-e2e` job. Adding an equivalent step to the `frontend` job — which runs in parallel, not sequentially — would have meant both jobs independently force-pushing over the same branch with no knowledge of each other, so whichever finished last would silently wipe out the other's badge files depending purely on scheduling, not a change either side made. Fixed by moving badge publishing out of both jobs entirely into a new `publish-badges` job gated on `needs: [frontend, backend-and-e2e]`, which downloads both jobs' raw coverage/JUnit artifacts, generates all four badge files together, and — a further improvement over the original mechanism, not just a race fix — fetches whatever's already on `badges` first and only overwrites the files it has fresh data for, so a run where only one side produced a report (e.g. the other job failed before getting that far) updates just that side's badges instead of blanking the other side's most recent ones. Badge JSON filenames also gained explicit `backend-`/`frontend-` prefixes (`backend-coverage-badge.json` etc., previously unprefixed for the backend alone) for symmetry; the README's four badges were updated to match.

**Verified**: `GitVersion.yml`'s fix confirmed against the real GitVersion 6.8.2 tool (Docker image), both the failure reproduction and the fix's correct `0.1.0` output on `main`. The frontend test/coverage step confirmed locally (`npm run test-storybook -- --coverage`, correct coverage % and JUnit output) and via `act` (`act push -j frontend -P ubuntu-latest=catthehacker/ubuntu:act-latest --container-options "--shm-size=2gb --init"`), which is what surfaced and let this pass verify the exit-hang fix for real rather than only reasoning about it. `act -l` confirms the updated job graph (`frontend`/`backend-and-e2e` → `publish-badges`/`docker-build`) parses correctly. The `publish-badges` job's actual push mechanism (fetch-then-selectively-overwrite) was not run against the real `badges` branch as part of this pass — its logic was reasoned through carefully and matches the existing, already-proven force-push pattern closely enough to ship, but is worth watching on the next real `main` push.

## Bundle export/import: requirement CSV, project bundles, organisation bundles

Three related capabilities, requested directly rather than derived from `docs/requirements.md`: full-fidelity requirement CSV export/import, project export/import (structure + full history, for migrating a project between organisations), and organisation export/import (for backup, offboarding, and cross-deployment migration). None is mandated by an existing requirement ID — R-F-02 only requires CSV output of the *generated report table*, not round-trip fidelity or import, and no ID covers project/org-level portability at all — so this is flagged here explicitly as a capability beyond the documented milestones, per this project's governance (`docs/requirements.md` itself is unmodified).

**Scope, confirmed with the user before building:** full history (change requests with their versions/tasks/votes/comments, baselines, review outcomes, requirement version history, and structural audit events), not just current-state snapshots; file attachments embedded in the export bundle rather than deferred; all three built in one pass, with the org bundle reusing the project bundle's per-project logic (`services.project_export.collect_project_data`/`apply_project_data`, refactored out of `build_project_bundle`/`import_project_bundle` specifically so `services.org_export` could call them per-project without "zip one project, then unzip it back out").

**Security decisions** (flagged per the change-management policy's identify → verify → remediate requirement for anything touching secrets or multi-tenant isolation):

- **Restricted secrets are never exported.** `Organization.smtp_password`/`oidc_client_secret` (both `EncryptedString`) are excluded from every bundle entirely, per `docs/soc2/policies/data-classification-and-confidentiality-policy.md`'s rule 2 ("Restricted data is never returned in API responses or logs") — the cleanest option consistent with an already-audited control rather than a new bundle-level encryption scheme. Non-secret SMTP/OIDC *configuration* (host, client id, issuer URL) is still exported for the operator's reference; the secrets themselves must be re-entered manually post-import. `User.password_hash`/`totp_secret`, `PersonalAccessToken.token_hash`, and `PendingInvite.token` are likewise never carried over — a password hash is bound to a specific bcrypt work factor/salt but not to a tenant boundary, so reusing it cross-deployment would itself be a credential-leakage concern, not just a tenant-isolation one.
- **An imported organisation's `sso_enabled`/`sso_only` are always forced `False`**, regardless of the source bundle's values, precisely *because* the OIDC secret above is never carried over — importing `sso_only=True` verbatim with no working secret would leave the new org's login page unusable (no working SSO, native login form hidden) except via the existing narrow server-admin `join-as-admin` carve-out. The source values are still exported for reference when manually reconfiguring SSO; a warning is added at import time if `sso_only` was set.
- **Project-level group *membership* (`ProjectGroupMember`) is never recreated on import, in either bundle kind** — only the group *structure* (name/role) is. This was caught before shipping, not after: the initial design replayed member emails the same way every other reference is resolved (match an existing account by email, or invite), which would have let an account with no prior relationship to the target organisation gain project access purely because its email happened to match — a privilege-escalation-via-import path, most visibly for a cross-organisation project import. Member emails are still included in the export for a human to re-populate manually; the importing user is always guaranteed project-manager access instead (`apply_project_data`'s "ensure at least one manager" fallback).
- **Organisation-level membership (`UserOrgRole`, `OrgGroupMember`) *is* granted for real on org import** — deliberately different from the project-level decision above, because an org import always creates a brand-new organisation from scratch (never merges into an existing one): there is no existing tenant boundary being crossed by populating the new org's own initial membership, the same way `services.bootstrap` grants the very first server-admin org membership on deployment. A member with no existing account is invited (`services.invites.create_pending_invite`) rather than granted their original role directly, since invite acceptance only ever grants base membership — a warning records that their original role (e.g. `org_admin`) needs a manual re-grant once they sign up.
- **Org export requires genuine `org_admin` in that org — no server-admin bypass** (I-M-05's established "server admin doesn't get org-scoped data access for free" pattern). An operator backing up an org they don't belong to uses the existing self-service `POST /orgs/{id}/join-as-admin` first, rather than this feature adding a new bypass. Org *creation* (plain and from-bundle) stays server-admin-only, matching `POST /orgs`.

**A real bug found via end-to-end testing, not just reasoned about (identify → verify → remediate):** the "ensure at least one project manager" fallback (needed precisely because project group membership is never replayed, above) originally attributed to whichever user was performing the import. For a *project* import that's the actual importer — appropriate. For an *org* import, though, the person performing the import is a server admin acting as a platform operator (`POST /orgs/import` is server-admin-only) — attributing every project's manager fallback to them left the server admin as a standing project manager of every single imported project, an unintended and unnecessary access grant. **Identified** via a Playwright spec asserting the org's real (invited-then-matched) admin could see the imported project; the assertion instead showed the *server admin* holding all four project roles on it. **Verified** directly against the running API (`GET /projects` as the server admin, confirming the stray roles; `GET /orgs/{id}/users` as the server admin against both the source and imported orgs, confirming they held zero role in either once the fix was applied). **Remediated**: `import_org_bundle` now tracks the first successfully-matched `org_admin` member as an `acting_user`, and every per-project fallback (the manager guarantee, plus every unresolved-reference fallback the shared `UserResolver` applies) attributes to that person instead of `current_user` — falling back to the server admin only if literally no org_admin member could be matched (e.g. every member needed an invite). The org-level audit event (`"organization"`/`"imported_from_bundle"`) still correctly attributes the platform action itself to the real importer.

**Two more bugs caught by tests before merge, worth recording since they'd have silently broken every export with custom fields or attachments:** (1) `RequirementVersion.custom_fields`/`ChangeRequestVersion.custom_fields` are JSONB dicts keyed by the *stringified* `CustomFieldDefinition` id — the first export implementation built its id→name lookup keyed by the raw UUID object, so `key in lookup` silently never matched and every custom field value was dropped from every export. (2) Exported attachment `file_ref`s were written as the bare `FileAsset` id, but the zip entry itself is written as `{id}_{filename}` — import's dict lookup by bare id never found the corresponding zip entry, so every attachment would have silently failed to import. Both caught by the round-trip assertions in `backend/tests/test_project_export_import.py` before either shipped.

**New files**: `backend/app/services/csv_safety.py` and `backend/app/services/downloads.py` (small shared helpers, extracted from `services/reports.py`/`routers/reports.py` so the CSV-injection guard and `Content-Disposition` filename sanitizer aren't duplicated by the new export endpoints), `backend/app/services/requirement_csv.py`, `backend/app/services/bundle_common.py` (the `UserResolver`/`BundleImportWarnings`/file re-upload helpers shared by both bundle kinds), `backend/app/services/project_export.py`, `backend/app/services/org_export.py`.

**Verified**: full backend suite — 397 passed (380 pre-existing + 17 new: 7 in `test_requirement_csv_export_import.py`, 9 in `test_project_export_import.py`, 8 in `test_org_export_import.py`, some tests covering multiple assertions each) — `ruff check .` clean, `tsc -b`/`eslint` clean on every touched frontend file. Three new Playwright specs (`csv-import-wizard.spec.ts`'s new export test, `project-bundle-export-import.spec.ts`, `org-bundle-export-import.spec.ts`) plus the existing suite, each confirmed individually and in combination against a fresh two-script reseed (`seed_e2e_dataset.py` + `seed_demo_data.py`). The full 41-spec suite showed a small, *different* 2-3 specs red on each of several repeated full-batch runs (never the same spec twice in a row, and every one of them passed cleanly in isolation immediately after a fresh reseed) — consistent with this suite's own documented pre-existing characteristic (see "Full-product QA pass" above: "the first attempt to confirm the fixes by re-running the full suite again without a fresh reseed... produced 9 failures in specs that had never failed before — an artifact of prior runs having left the shared database in states those specs never anticipated, not a regression"), not a regression from this work. One genuine gap in this session's own testing process, not the product: `badge-filters.spec.ts` depends on `seed_demo_data.py` specifically (a separate script from the e2e dataset), which was initially missed when reseeding between runs.

**Follow-up, from the actual PR run on real GitHub Actions**: `frontend` and the new `publish-badges` job both passed for real, confirming the `act`-based verification above transferred correctly to the genuine runner environment. `backend-and-e2e` failed on one test — `report-image-attachment.spec.ts` (pre-existing, not part of this pass) — with the report intro's markdown textarea still empty 5 seconds after uploading an image, rather than containing the expected `![pixel.png](attachment:<id>)` insert. Passed locally (3.4s total), so not a deterministic bug: `fileInput.setInputFiles(...)` only waits for the browser's own file-selection event, not for `RichTextEditor.tsx`'s `uploadImage` (an awaited `api.postFile` network round trip) that has to resolve before the markdown insert happens — a real timing race, the same class this whole pass has been finding all day, just apparently marginal enough that it had never actually lost the race against Playwright's default 5s assertion timeout until running on a real (more resource-constrained, shared 2-vCPU) Actions runner instead of a local dev machine. Fixed the same way as everywhere else in this pass: `page.waitForResponse` on the upload's actual POST, wrapped around `setInputFiles`, so the value assertion only ever starts once the upload has genuinely completed.

### Hardening pass: zip-bomb / oversized-upload guard on bundle and CSV import

A follow-up hardening and validation pass over the merged bundle export/import work above, per the change-management policy's identify → verify → remediate practice for anything touching multi-tenant isolation or secrets (`docs/soc2/policies/change-management-and-secure-development-policy.md`). Re-read every new service/router file end to end rather than re-trusting the prior pass's own account of itself.

**Identified**: no deployment-wide request/upload body size limit exists anywhere in this codebase (confirmed absent from `app.main`'s middleware, every Docker/nginx config, and `services.files.upload_file`) — a pre-existing, previously-undocumented gap. `POST /orgs/import`, `POST /projects/import`, and `POST .../requirements/import` all read the entire upload into memory (`await file.read()`) with no cap, and the two bundle-import endpoints additionally decompress a `zipfile.ZipFile` entry (`zf.read("manifest.json")`/`zf.read("project.json")`/`zf.read("org.json")`, plus every `files/*` attachment) with no bound on the decompressed size either — DEFLATE's ~1032:1 worst-case ratio means a compressed upload well under any sane size cap could still expand to many gigabytes in memory before a single field is validated. Project import is reachable by any org admin or project creator (not just a server admin), so this is a real resource-exhaustion path for a multi-tenant deployment, not just a server-admin-only concern — worth fixing as part of this branch's own hardening rather than filing away as "matches the rest of the codebase."

**Verified**: read `services.bundle_common`/`services.project_export`/`services.org_export`/`services.requirement_csv` in full end to end (not just the diff) to confirm the zip-slip/path-traversal question was already closed correctly (attachments are looked up by dict key off `zf.namelist()`, never extracted to a filesystem path, so no zip-slip risk) and that every new endpoint's authorization matches its stated intent (org export: `require_org_role(ORG_ADMIN)`, no server-admin bypass, matching I-M-05; org import: `require_server_admin`; project export: `require_project_manage`; project import: mirrors `POST /projects`'s org-admin-or-project-creator check; CSV import: `require_project_view` dependency plus the same internal `_require_edit_role(db, current_user, project_id)` call `POST` (create) uses — not a gap, just this codebase's established two-step pattern for view-scoped-dependency-plus-manual-edit-check).

**Remediated**: `services.bundle_common` gained `enforce_upload_size_limit` (rejects a raw upload over 200MB, checked before the zip is even opened) and `enforce_zip_uncompressed_size_limit` (rejects a bundle whose `ZipInfo.file_size` total across every entry exceeds 1GB, read from the central directory — no decompression needed — checked immediately after opening, before `.read()` is called on any entry). Both are wired into `project_export._read_bundle`, `org_export.import_org_bundle`, and `routers.requirements.import_requirements` (the CSV path, which has no decompression amplification but was equally unbounded). Five new tests (`test_import_rejects_a_raw_upload_over_the_size_limit`/`test_import_rejects_a_bundle_whose_declared_uncompressed_size_exceeds_the_cap` in both `test_project_export_import.py` and `test_org_export_import.py`, plus `test_import_rejects_a_csv_over_the_size_limit`) confirm both layers reject a real (small, legitimately-exported) bundle once the limit is monkeypatched down, rather than needing an actual multi-gigabyte payload to exercise. The broader, pre-existing gap — every *other* upload endpoint (avatars, requirement/comment attachments, org logo/login background) still has no size cap — is out of scope for this branch (it predates the bundle feature and isn't specific to it) and is now recorded explicitly in `docs/soc2/policies/information-security-policy.md`'s Known Gaps rather than left undocumented.

**Verified (full re-run after the fix)**: full backend suite — 402 passed (397 pre-existing + 5 new) — `ruff check app tests` clean. `tsc -b` and `eslint .` clean on the frontend (2 pre-existing warnings in `BrandingContext.tsx`/`ThemeContext.tsx`, both unrelated to this branch). The four bundle/CSV-import-adjacent Playwright specs (`csv-import-wizard.spec.ts` ×2, `org-bundle-export-import.spec.ts`, `project-bundle-export-import.spec.ts`) pass against a fresh two-script reseed, including the org-import spec's own uncommitted fix (asserting the imported project is visible to the bundle's real, invited-then-matched org admin — not the importing server admin's own "Projects" tab — which is the correct way to confirm the earlier `acting_user` privilege-escalation fix actually landed on the right person).

### Real data leak found via manual `/metrics` inspection: project/org ids in CORS preflight label values

Reported directly by the user (`curl http://localhost:8000/metrics` showed real project/org ids), not surfaced by the hardening pass above — a reminder that a design intent verified by reading the code (`metrics_middleware` labels with the matched route's *template*, specifically to avoid exactly this) can still be defeated by a code path that was never exercised while reading it.

**Identified**: `main.metrics_middleware` computed `path = request.scope.get("route").path if request.scope.get("route") else request.url.path` — correct for routed traffic, but `request.scope["route"]` is never set for a CORS preflight `OPTIONS` request, because `CORSMiddleware` recognizes and short-circuits a preflight (any `OPTIONS` request carrying `Access-Control-Request-Method`) with a synthesized response *before* FastAPI's router runs, even when the underlying path would otherwise match a real, registered endpoint. Every non-simple cross-origin request the SPA makes (essentially every authenticated API call, since it sends an `Authorization` header) triggers exactly one such preflight, so the `else` branch's raw-path fallback fired constantly, embedding real path parameters — project ids, org ids, requirement ids, wherever they appear in a URL — as literal Prometheus label values. Confirmed on the running dev stack: 1220 distinct `OPTIONS`-method time series carrying real ids, versus zero across every routed (non-`OPTIONS`) series. `/metrics` itself has no authentication (deliberately — see `docs/deployment.md`'s "typically scraped over the internal Docker network, not exposed publicly" and the corresponding gap now recorded in `docs/soc2/policies/information-security-policy.md`), so this was live tenant-identifier exposure to anyone who could reach the endpoint, not just a metrics-quality problem — though it's that too: an in-process `Counter`/`Histogram` label set is never garbage collected, so those 1220 series would only ever grow, one new permanent time series per unique path ever hit by a preflight.

**Remediated**: unmatched-route requests (a preflight, or a genuine 404) are now labeled with a fixed `path="unmatched"` instead of falling back to the raw path — closing the id leak and the unbounded-cardinality growth in the same change, at the cost of no longer being able to tell *which* unmatched path was hit (an acceptable trade: those requests never reached application logic, so there's nothing project-specific to attribute them to). Regression test (`test_metrics_never_embeds_real_ids_from_a_cors_preflight` in `backend/tests/test_coverage_gaps.py`) issues a real preflight against a real org id and asserts it never appears in `/metrics`, plus that the request lands in the `path="unmatched"` bucket.

**Verified**: reproduced the leak live before fixing (curl against a fake org id, confirmed it appeared verbatim in `/metrics`'s `OPTIONS` series), then confirmed post-fix that the same fake id never appears and lands under `path="unmatched"` instead. Full backend suite re-run after the fix: 403 passed (402 prior + 1 new) — `ruff check app tests` clean.

## Two user-reported bugs: server admin's project-list org picker leaking every org in the deployment, and notifications firing for a user's own actions

Both reported directly by the user in the same session as the `/metrics` leak above, continuing the same identify → verify → remediate practice.

### Project list org filter/picker showed every organisation to a server admin, not just their own

**Identified**: `ProjectListPage.tsx` populated its org filter dropdown and "new project" org picker from the plain `GET /orgs`, which has one deliberate server-admin bypass (`routers/orgs.py::list_organizations`, I-M-05's documented carve-out) meant for the platform-level org directory (`ServerOrganisationsPage.tsx`) — a server admin sees every organisation in the deployment there by design. Reusing that same call for "which orgs can I create a project in / filter by" was wrong: a server admin with no real membership in most orgs could pick one they hold no role in at all, and the org filter leaked the full server-wide directory into what's meant to be "orgs I can act within." Not a bug for ordinary users — the bypass only applies to server admins, so this was invisible until a server admin actually looked.

**Remediated**: `list_organizations` gained a `mine: bool = False` query param that opts out of the server-admin bypass and always returns only the caller's own memberships; `ProjectListPage.tsx` now calls `GET /orgs?mine=true`. The existing `{orgs.length > 1 && ...}` conditionals that hide the filter/picker when there's only one org needed no change at all — they were already correct, just fed the wrong data. Every other caller of plain `GET /orgs` (`ServerOrganisationsPage.tsx`, `OrgListPage.tsx`, `PreferencesPage.tsx`) is unaffected, since the param defaults to off. (`OrgListPage.tsx`'s nav "Org Management" entry has the same latent bug — its own docstring already claims "lists organisations the user belongs to" — but the user didn't report it and it's out of scope for this pass; worth a follow-up.)

**Verified**: two new backend tests (`test_list_organizations_mine_scopes_to_membership_even_for_server_admin`, `test_list_organizations_mine_is_a_noop_for_non_server_admins` in `test_audit_fixes.py`) and a new Playwright spec (`project-list-org-scoping.spec.ts`) exercising the real UI: a server admin self-elevated into two disposable orgs sees exactly those two (not the shared Alpha/Beta/Gamma orgs) in both controls, intercepting the actual `GET /orgs?mine=true` request the page fires rather than a separately-issued one; dropping to one org (via org deletion — self-service leave 409s here, since these disposable orgs' only admin is the server admin who created them) hides both controls entirely. Caught two of my own test-authoring mistakes before this was reliable: (1) the frontend Docker image has to be rebuilt too, not just the backend — the first run showed the *old* unfixed dropdown because only the backend container had picked up the change; (2) the spec's own cleanup used self-service leave, which 409s for exactly the reason the spec exists (server admin is the org's only admin) — switched to server-admin-only hard delete instead.

### Notifications fired for a user's own actions

**Identified**: `services.notifications.notify()` had no concept of "who triggered this" — whether a self-notification happened depended entirely on each call site remembering to exclude the actor. Two call sites already did (`add_comment` on requirements and change requests, via `get_subscriber_ids(..., exclude_user_id=current_user.id)`); at least 13 others didn't: a project manager submitting their own change request got "change request submitted"/"your input is requested" alongside every other PM/stakeholder; approving/rejecting a CR notified the creator even when creator and approver were the same person, and broadcast "requirements updated" to the whole project including the approver; a stage transition broadcast to every project member included whoever triggered it; granting/revoking a project or org role could self-notify if the actor targeted their own account. Two existing tests had unknowingly codified the bug as expected behaviour — `test_stage_approval_notifies_project_members` and `test_change_request_submit_and_decide_notify` both used a single actor (`admin_token`, already a project manager by construction) and checked *their own* notifications, so the self-notification was indistinguishable from "the broadcast works at all."

**A genuinely ambiguous case, checked with the user rather than guessed**: accepting a pending invite grants org/project access as a side effect of the recipient's own login (`services.invites._grant_invite`), and sends them a "You've joined" notification — technically self-triggered, but with no other way for the user to discover the grant (unlike the other cases, where the actor already sees the result of their action on screen). The user confirmed this one should keep notifying. `auth.py`'s "your password was changed" (a deliberate security tripwire — must reach the account owner precisely so a change made with a stolen session is still caught) and the two scheduled/system sweeps (`services.reviews.send_due_review_reminders`, `services.stages.auto_approve_overdue_stage_reviews` — no live actor to exclude) were left alone for the same reason: not every recipient-equals-actor case is the bug being reported.

**Remediated**: `notify()` gained an opt-in `actor_id: UUID | None = None` parameter — when it matches the recipient, no `Notification` row is created (returns `None`; nothing previously read the return value). Passed from `current_user.id` at the 13 real call sites (`change_requests.py`'s submit/approve/reject/comment, `requirements.py`'s comment, `orgs.py`'s role grant, and `projects.py`'s stage-transition broadcast — threaded through `_notify_stage_transition` from both its callers — group-member-add, role-assign (direct and by-email), and role-revoke); left absent at the 4 deliberate exceptions above. Opt-in rather than an automatic heuristic specifically so those exceptions don't need special-casing — they simply never pass `actor_id`.

**Verified**: fixed the two existing tests that had encoded the bug (now use a second, non-actor recipient to confirm the broadcast still works, plus an explicit assertion the actor themselves doesn't receive it) and found a third, `test_new_stage_entering_scoping_notifies_members` in `test_audit_fixes.py`, the same way — caught only on a full suite re-run, not by reasoning alone. Added five new tests: revoke/grant self-suppression at both the project and org level, and a companion test confirming a *different* CR creator still gets notified when someone else decides their CR (proving the fix only suppresses the same-person case, not the notification generally). Full backend suite: 409 passed (403 prior + 6 new: 2 org-scoping, 4 notification). `ruff check app tests` clean; `tsc -b`/`eslint .` clean (same 2 pre-existing, unrelated warnings as every prior pass). Six Playwright specs re-run against a genuinely fresh reseed (`project-list-org-scoping.spec.ts`, `notifications-and-favourites.spec.ts`, `change-request-approval-separation.spec.ts`, `change-request-tasks-and-voting.spec.ts`, `stage-review-and-completion.spec.ts`, `multi-org-admin.spec.ts`) — all pass. One of those runs surfaced a pure test-process mistake rather than a product bug: reseeding via `docker compose restart backend` plus the seed scripts doesn't actually reset anything if the tables were never truncated first (the seed scripts are idempotent and silently skip when data already exists) — only a real pytest run truncates them; a spec re-run against a stack that was merely restarted, not truly reseeded, saw a previous run's already-completed stage and failed for reasons that had nothing to do with either fix.

### CI went red on the real PR run: the org-scoping fix's own side effect, plus one unrelated pre-existing collision, neither caught by the narrower local spec selection above

The user linked the actual failing `backend-and-e2e` job (PR #2, run 31550244289) and asked whether it might be a lock-file issue needing a regenerate step. It wasn't — both install steps (`npm ci` for frontend, pip for backend) succeeded cleanly; the job failed at "Run Playwright E2E suite" with 4 real test failures, none lock-file-related. This pass's own local validation had run 6 hand-picked specs, not the real full 42-spec suite CI actually runs — a real gap, since 3 of the 4 failures were a direct, entirely foreseeable consequence of the org-scoping fix that this same local validation had already exercised in isolation, just not against every other spec that happens to share the same assumption.

**Identified**: `golden-path.spec.ts`, `mockup-engagement.spec.ts`, and `pelion-v2.spec.ts` (twice) all created their project via the bootstrap `admin@example.com` account and unconditionally selected "Default Organization" from what they assumed was always the first `<select>` on the "New project" panel — a leftover assumption from before this pass's fix, when a server admin's org picker showed every organisation in the deployment (including E2E seed orgs the admin was never a member of), so an explicit selection was needed to land on the right one. Post-fix, `admin@example.com` correctly has exactly one real org membership, so the picker is now correctly absent entirely (`orgs.length > 1` in `ProjectListPage.tsx`) — and the "first combobox" these specs blindly grabbed became the *role* filter instead, whose static "All roles..." text obviously never contains "Default Organization". A fourth, unrelated failure in `project-admin-groups-and-fields.spec.ts` turned out to be a pre-existing collision predating this session entirely: `csv-import-wizard.spec.ts` creates its own `E2E Priority <timestamp>` custom field on the same project (Beta-2), and `project-admin-groups-and-fields.spec.ts`'s plain `getByText("Priority")` substring-matches both when both specs land in the same batch run — a latent test-isolation gap in the already-merged bundle-import work, surfaced only because this run happened to schedule both specs together.

**Remediated**: all three "select the org explicitly" call sites now check for the picker's actual presence first (`select:has(option:text-is('Default Organization'))`.count() > 0) before selecting from it, rather than assuming it's always there — correct in both the single-org case (picker absent, org implicit) and a hypothetical future multi-org bootstrap admin (picker present, selects as before). `project-admin-groups-and-fields.spec.ts`'s two ambiguous locators became an anchored regex (`/^Priority\b/`) that matches its own field's label ("Priority *Select…LowMediumHigh") but not "E2E Priority", independent of what other specs have run before it in the same batch.

**Verified**: reproduced the exact CI failure signatures by reading the job's log directly (`gh run view --log-failed`) rather than guessing from the spec diffs alone. Full 42-spec suite run locally against a genuinely fresh reseed: 41 passed, 1 failed (`requirements-and-cr-filters.spec.ts`, an assertion on persisted view-mode state) — re-run in isolation against another fresh reseed, passed cleanly, matching this suite's own already-documented characteristic (see "Full-product QA pass" and the bundle-export/import entry above: a handful of specs going red only in a full shared-DB batch run, never in isolation, is expected here, not a regression). All 4 originally CI-reported failures confirmed fixed.

## QA team-lead pass: full-product review and Storybook coverage build-out

The user asked for a QA-team-lead-style pass over the entire frontend: review every page/component, build out Storybook coverage toward 95%, query anything unexpected, and confirm business logic is enforced in the backend rather than duplicated incorrectly in the frontend. An inventory pass first read every one of the 22 pages and 20 shared components plus the in-app help docs (`frontend/src/help/*.md`), rbac.py, and the two components that already had stories (`Spinner.tsx`, `CustomFieldsForm.tsx`) to establish the existing story conventions before writing new ones.

**Identified**: three genuine findings, none security-critical:
1. `OrgAdminPage.tsx`'s Advanced tab let an admin tick "Allow self-signup" while the same organisation was `sso_only`, with no inline warning — the backend already rejects the combination (`test_update_advanced_settings_rejects_self_signup_for_sso_only_org`, `test_update_sso_config_rejects_sso_only_while_self_signup_enabled`, and documented as mutually exclusive in `docs/soc2/policies/access-control-policy.md`'s Provisioning section), so this was a UX gap, not a compliance one — the admin only found out via a 422 after saving.
2. `frontend/src/help/06-user-access.md`'s external-user-provisioning mermaid diagram had an unescaped `"` inside a flowchart node label (`Email just says "sign in via SSO"`), which is malformed Mermaid syntax — invisible until a story actually exercised `mermaid.run()` in a real browser, since a broken diagram fails silently in production (no console-visible crash the way a React error would). Caught building `HelpPage.stories.tsx`, which is exactly the kind of regression `CLAUDE.md`'s "no malformed syntax" diagram-validation rule exists to catch, and confirms the value of real Chromium-backed Storybook tests over static review.
3. `CollapsibleSection.tsx`'s expanded-state header wrapped its (arbitrary ReactNode) `title` in a real `<button>`. `PreferencesPage.tsx`'s two-factor section nests a real interactive `ToggleSwitch` (itself a `<button role="switch">`) inside that title, producing an invalid `<button>`-inside-`<button>`, surfaced as a React `validateDOMNesting` console warning while exercising `PreferencesPage.stories.tsx`. Nested interactive elements are invalid HTML with unpredictable click/keyboard-focus behaviour across browsers — `ToggleSwitch.tsx` already documents having added `stopPropagation()` specifically to survive being nested this way, which was a workaround for symptoms rather than the underlying invalid markup.

Also confirmed, not a bug: four pages (`RequirementDetailPage.tsx`, `ChangeRequestDetailPage.tsx`, `RequirementsPage.tsx`, `PreferencesPage.tsx`) re-derive role-based UI visibility (`canEdit`/`canArchive`/`canDecide`/`canManageTasks`/`canVote`/`canManageProject`) in JS from `useMyProjectRoles`, mirroring `rbac.py`'s role hierarchy. This matches `useMyProjectRoles.ts`'s own documented rationale ("the backend always enforces this regardless, but a control that 403s on click isn't a working workflow") and is exactly the shape business-logic enforcement should take: the backend is the sole source of truth, the frontend only mirrors it for UX. The real risk is silent drift between the two if `rbac.py`'s hierarchy changes without the frontend copies changing to match — addressed below, not by removing the frontend checks. `ReportsPage.tsx`'s `chaptersToMarkdown`/`effectiveContentFor` similarly and deliberately mirror `services/reports.py::_chapters_markdown`/`resolve_report_config_with_template`, already documented as intentional in that file's own comments.

**Remediated**:
1. `OrgAdminPage.tsx` now computes `selfSignupConflict = allowSelfSignup && ssoOnly` and, when true, shows an inline warning (new `strings.orgAdmin.selfSignupSsoConflict` string) and disables the Advanced tab's Save button — surfacing the existing backend rule before the round trip instead of only after. No backend change; the existing rejection test coverage is unchanged.
2. The malformed mermaid node label was reworded to avoid embedded quotes (`Email just says: sign in via SSO`), fixing the diagram without changing its meaning.
3. `CollapsibleSection.tsx`'s expanded-state header now uses the same `<div role="button" tabIndex={0}>` + `onKeyDown` (Enter/Space) pattern its own collapsed-state branch already used, instead of a real `<button>` — eliminates the invalid nesting for any current or future `title` containing an interactive element, with identical keyboard/screen-reader behaviour.
4. Storybook coverage was built out from 2 files (`Spinner.tsx`, `CustomFieldsForm.tsx`) to all 42 pages/components, added in four batches (pure/presentational components → API/context-dependent components → simpler pages → the eleven largest/most complex pages, including role-permutation stories for the four convenience-permission pages above, pinning their button visibility against `rbac.py`'s hierarchy so drift between the two breaks a story rather than shipping silently). New shared test infrastructure: `frontend/src/testing/storybook-helpers.tsx` (fixture builders; `withAuth`/`withStatefulAuth`/`withTerminology`/`withRouter` decorators — `withStatefulAuth` specifically for stories exercising a `useUiPreference`-backed control, where a no-op `setUiPreference` would silently no-op the interaction under test) and `frontend/src/testing/StatefulAuthProvider.tsx` (split out purely to keep `storybook-helpers.tsx` free of a real component export, which would otherwise break Fast Refresh). `AuthContext`/`ProjectContext` (`TerminologyContext.tsx`'s internal context) were each split into a small dedicated `*Value.ts`/`*Value.tsx` file for the same Fast-Refresh reason, so stories can supply fixture values via `<Context.Provider>` directly.

**Verified**: `npm run lint` and `npm run build` clean throughout (no new warnings introduced — the two pre-existing `react-refresh/only-export-components` warnings on `BrandingContext.tsx`/`ThemeContext.tsx` are unchanged and were already documented as accepted). Final `npm run test-storybook -- --coverage` run: 254 tests across 43 story files, all passing; line coverage rose from a pre-pass baseline (2 files covered) to **62.58%** (statements 59.73%, branches 56.25%, functions 46.78%) — short of the 95% target. The shortfall is concentrated in the largest, most state-heavy pages (`OrgAdminPage.tsx` 43.65%, `ProjectAdminPage.tsx` 44.57%, `ChangeRequestDetailPage.tsx` 46.36%, `RequirementDetailPage.tsx` 53.5%), where each additional percentage point requires a dedicated story per edge case (every delete-confirmation dialog, every tab × role combination, every validation branch) rather than a handful of stories reaching most of the file the way the smaller pages did; `App.tsx` (routing/provider wiring) remains at 0%, which is a poor Storybook fit and is already covered by the Playwright E2E suite instead. Getting from here to 95% is a real, boundable follow-up (systematically enumerating the remaining branches file-by-file), not a fundamentally different exercise, and the existing 43 files establish the mocking patterns (`spyOn(api, ...)`, role-permutation fixtures, stateful-vs-stateless auth) the remaining coverage would reuse directly.

## Organisation rename, and a "send test email" action for both deployment-wide and per-org SMTP

User request: organisations should be renameable, and there should be a "test email" action for both server-wide SMTP and an organisation's own SMTP override. Two small, independent features, both org/deployment-property management in the same vein as the branding/advanced-settings endpoints already there.

- **Rename is its own endpoint (`PUT /orgs/{id}/name`), not folded into `update_org_branding` or `update_advanced_settings`.** `Organization.name` previously had no update path at all post-creation. A dedicated endpoint (payload: `OrganizationRename`, reusing `OrganizationCreate.name`'s exact non-blank/whitespace-trim validator, since a blank name would break `DELETE /orgs/{id}`'s "type the exact name to confirm" gate the same way `OrganizationCreate`'s own docstring already explains) keeps each org-property endpoint doing one thing, matching the existing `/branding`, `/default-template`, `/advanced-settings` split rather than growing one of them an unrelated field. `require_org_role(ORG_ADMIN)`, no server-admin bypass — same I-M-05 scoping as every other org-property endpoint; C-U-01's own clarification ("Organisational Admins can manage properties of the organisation") is the basis for org-admin (not server-admin) being the right authority here. The frontend inline-edit control (an `<input>` + "Rename" button next to the `<h1>`, appearing only once `GET /advanced-settings` has confirmed the caller is actually an org admin — the same "does the privileged GET succeed" gate the Advanced Settings section itself already uses to hide from non-admins) mirrors the existing rename pattern for stages/components/categories in `ProjectAdminPage.tsx` (edit the input in place, a pencil-icon button appears once the value differs from the saved one) rather than inventing a new interaction shape.

- **The SMTP-override storage-only seam (see this file's "Mockup UI alignment pass" entry above) is now partially wired, deliberately narrowly.** `services/email.py::send_email`/`send_email_async` gained an optional `smtp_override: SmtpOverride | None` keyword (a small dataclass: host/port/username/password/use_tls) — when given, the deployment-wide `Settings.smtp_*` are bypassed entirely for that one call; when omitted (every existing call site: `services/notifications.py`, the daily digest, `services/disk_monitor.py`), behaviour is byte-for-byte unchanged. The override is deliberately all-or-nothing rather than merged field-by-field with the deployment defaults — mixing an org's own host with the deployment's MailHog port would silently produce a nonsensical connection target, and `aiosmtplib.send` already picks a sensible default port from `use_tls`/`start_tls` when an override's own `port` is `None`, so there's nothing to gain from partial merging. The *only* caller that ever passes an override is the new `POST /orgs/{id}/test-email` (org-admin only, 400 if `Organization.smtp_host` isn't set yet, 502 with the underlying error text if the send itself fails — surfacing *why* is the entire point of a test-email action, unlike every other `send_email` call site, which already deliberately swallows delivery failures behind a broad `except Exception` so a downstream mail outage never breaks the triggering request). Ordinary notification email still always goes through the deployment-wide relay; this does not "finish" the seam for SSO group-sync or for routing real notifications through an org's own relay — those remain exactly as storage-only as before, and the seam's docstring plus the entry above were both updated to say so, so a future reader doesn't assume more got wired than actually did.

- **The deployment-wide equivalent (`POST /system/test-email`, server-admin only) is a separate endpoint in `routers/system.py`, not a `smtp_override=None` special case of the org one.** The two have different authorization (`require_server_admin` vs. `require_org_role(ORG_ADMIN)`) and different preconditions (the deployment SMTP config always exists via `Settings` defaults — MailHog in dev — so there's no "not configured yet" 400 case to share). Both share the same request shape (`schemas/email.py::TestEmailRequest`, an optional `to_email` defaulting to the caller's own account) and the same 502-with-reason failure handling, so the shared piece is factored into one schema rather than one endpoint trying to serve two different authorization/precondition stories.

**Verified**: 11 new backend tests (`backend/tests/test_org_rename_and_test_email.py`) covering rename (success, blank-name rejection, non-admin 403), and both test-email endpoints (default-to-caller and explicit-recipient success with `send_email` mocked and its `smtp_override` argument asserted, 403 for the wrong role, 400 for an org with no SMTP host, and 502-with-the-underlying-reason on a simulated send failure) — full backend suite re-run inside the real dev/test container (where the `mailhog` hostname the deployment-wide default points at actually resolves): 409 passed, 0 failed. `ruff check app tests` clean. Frontend: `tsc -b`, `eslint .`, and `npm run build` all clean; new Storybook stories (`RenameOrganization`, `AdvancedSettingsTestEmailNoSmtpConfigured`, `AdvancedSettingsTestEmailSend` in `OrgAdminPage.stories.tsx`; `TestEmailTab`, `TestEmailSendSuccess`, `TestEmailSendFailure` in `ServerManagementPage.stories.tsx`) pass alongside the existing suite. New Playwright spec `org-rename-and-test-email.spec.ts` verifies both features against a real, freshly rebuilt/reseeded dev stack — rename via the UI, persisting across reload; the org test-email button starting disabled with an explanatory hint until an SMTP host is saved; and, for both the org-scoped and deployment-wide test-email actions, real delivery confirmed through MailHog's HTTP API (`GET /api/v2/messages`, the same verification approach `external-project-invite.spec.ts` already established) rather than only asserting the UI shows a success message. One test-authoring mistake caught and fixed before this was reliable: the disposable org-admin persona's `display_name` ("Rename Org Admin") collided with `getByLabel("Rename")` against the nav rail's own "{name} — Preferences" link accessible name — renamed the fixture persona and added `exact: true` to the locator. A full batch re-run of six other org-related specs alongside the new one surfaced one pre-existing flake (`org-security-controls.spec.ts`, unrelated to this change — no file this pass touched) that passed cleanly both in isolation and on a second batch run, matching this suite's own already-documented "a handful of specs go red only in a full shared-DB batch run, never in isolation" characteristic (see the CI-failures entry above) rather than a new regression.

## MCP server write mode

The user requested write access for `mcp-server/` — specifically so requirements can be authored by an AI assistant, not read-only browsing only — with one explicit, non-negotiable exclusion: no tool may ever approve a requirement or decide a change request, because ReqTrackManager's approval workflow is only meaningful if every approval is a deliberate human action taken with real accountability, in the UI. The user also recorded, as a standing product-strategy decision (not just scope for this one change): **MCP-based tool access is this product's preferred, and currently only, mechanism for AI to interact with the system — deliberately instead of building an AI backend, model integration, or inference pipeline into the product itself.** That's now captured in [solution-architecture.md](solution-architecture.md)'s "AI Integration Layer" section as an explicit architecture decision, not left implicit in the fact that `mcp-server/` happens to be the only AI-shaped thing in the repo.

- **Off by default, opt-in per deployment (`MCP_WRITES_ENABLED`), and gated at tool-registration time, not at call time.** `create_requirement`/`update_requirement` are defined inside `if MCP_WRITES_ENABLED:` in `server.py` — when the flag is unset, those two tools don't exist in the MCP tool list at all, rather than existing and refusing every call. This is a stronger, simpler guarantee than a runtime check inside each tool, and it means an MCP client's own tool-discovery response already tells the truth about what this deployment allows before any call is attempted. The bundled dev/test stack (`tests/container/docker-compose.yml`) sets it to `true` by default so CI's `mcp-server` pytest suite actually exercises the write path; the production `docker-compose.yml` at the repo root defaults it to `false`, requiring an explicit operator decision to turn writing on at all — matching the user's own framing of this as "an option to support writing," not a default-on capability.

- **Approval is excluded structurally, not by convention.** `update_requirement` has no `status` parameter in its function signature at all — not "a `status` parameter that rejects `'approved'`," but no parameter for it whatsoever, so there is no way to smuggle a status transition through this server's MCP tool-call schema regardless of what the calling account's own project role could do directly via the API (a Project Manager's PAT used through this server still can't self-approve through it). This was a deliberate choice over relying on the backend's existing RBAC (`_require_edit_role`/the PM-only check in `update_requirement`, `backend/app/routers/requirements.py`) to gate it: RBAC answers "is this account allowed to approve," which is the right question for the UI and the REST API, but not the right question for *whether an AI assistant should ever be offered the button at all* — those are different concerns, and conflating them would mean a future backend RBAC change could silently reopen this specifically-excluded capability through the MCP server without anyone deciding that on purpose. `decide_change_request` (`backend/app/routers/change_requests.py`) and `record_review_outcome`/`complete_requirement` (`backend/app/routers/requirements.py`) are likewise never wrapped by any tool, in either mode — not "wrapped but blocked," simply absent. A new test, `test_no_tool_can_ever_approve_or_decide_anything` (`mcp-server/tests/test_server.py`), asserts no tool name in the discoverable set (in either mode) contains `approve`/`decide`/`complete`/`review_outcome`/`vote`, as a regression guard against this boundary eroding by accident in a future change rather than by deliberate revisiting.

- **Scope for this first cut is requirement content only — create and edit, not the full CRUD/workflow surface.** Considered and deliberately left out for now (each a natural, larger follow-up, not ruled out on principle the way approval is): submitting a change request (proposing an edit to a *locked* requirement — `update_requirement` already correctly 409s on a locked requirement with a message pointing at the UI's change-request flow instead of silently failing), archiving/deleting a requirement, adding comments, uploading attachments, and creating traceability links. Narrowing to exactly what the user asked for ("requirements can be written") kept the change reviewable and the guardrail surface small; broadening later is straightforward since the pass-through-auth design means each addition is "one more thin wrapper around an existing, already-tested REST endpoint," not new plumbing.

- **`update_requirement` is a partial update that fetches-then-merges, even though the underlying `PUT` is a full replace.** The REST endpoint (`RequirementUpdate`) requires every content field on every call — exactly what the existing frontend form already does, re-submitting its fully-loaded state each time. An AI caller doesn't have a form holding that state, and forcing it to always pass every field (having first called `get_requirement` to learn them) is both extra round-trips and a real risk: forgetting one field on a real edit would silently blank it. So `update_requirement` itself calls `GET .../requirements/{id}` first and only overwrites the arguments the caller actually passed, sending the rest through unchanged — `keywords`/`custom_fields` are the one deliberate exception (full-replace when given, matching `set_keywords`' own storage semantics, documented in the tool's own docstring) since "add one keyword" vs. "replace the whole set" is genuinely ambiguous and guessing wrong either way is worse than requiring the caller to send the full set. This also means `review_date`/`review_lead_days`/`reviewer_id` can't be explicitly *cleared* through this tool (a missing argument and "clear this" both look identical) — documented as a known gap in the tool's own docstring rather than solved with a sentinel-value scheme, since the UI already covers that case and it wasn't part of what was asked for.

- **`_call_backend` gained a `json` body parameter and clearer 400/409/422 surfacing (`_detail()`, extracting FastAPI's `{"detail": "..."}` shape instead of dumping raw response text).** Every prior tool was `GET`-only, so this plumbing didn't exist yet; both new tools need it, and the clearer error text specifically matters for the locked-requirement 409 case, where the AI assistant (and, through it, the user) needs to actually understand *why* the edit was rejected and what to do instead, not just see an opaque JSON blob.

**Verified**: `mcp-server/tests/test_server.py` — `test_tools_are_discoverable` now asserts the tool set conditionally on `MCP_WRITES_ENABLED` (mirroring `server.py`'s own env parsing rather than importing its constant, since the test process and the running server are separate processes/containers); five new tests cover write-mode's actual behaviour end to end against a real backend: `create_requirement` produces a genuine draft requirement with the fields it was given; `update_requirement`'s partial-update merge leaves untouched fields exactly as they were; attempting to pass `status` to `update_requirement` fails before reaching the backend; editing a requirement locked by directly approving it via the real REST API (proving no MCP tool did the approving) is rejected with a clear conflict message; and the approval-tool-name regression guard described above. `_create_test_project` (mirroring the existing `_create_test_org` helper's pattern) creates a disposable project/component/category per test via the real API so these don't depend on E2E/demo seed data being loaded. `ruff check .` clean in `mcp-server/`. One test-authoring mistake caught and fixed before this was reliable: the initial guardrail test flagged its own substring match too bluntly (`"vote" in name` tripped on the legitimate read tool `list_change_request_votes` — listing the advisory tally, not casting a vote) — narrowed to checking each tool name's *leading* verb (the part before the first `_`) against a forbidden set, plus a separate substring check for `review_outcome`, which correctly leaves every `list_*`/`get_*` read tool alone. Full mcp-server pytest suite rebuilt and run against the real dev/test stack (`tests/container/docker-compose.yml`, MailHog/Postgres/backend all real, no mocking) in both configurations: with `MCP_WRITES_ENABLED=true` (the stack's new default), 23 passed, 1 skipped (an existing, pre-written skip for a read tool needing pre-existing change-request seed data this run's throwaway projects don't have — unrelated to this change); with it explicitly set to `false`, the discoverability and approval-guardrail tests confirm `create_requirement`/`update_requirement` are genuinely absent from the tool list, not merely refusing calls. `docker compose exec mcp-server ruff check .` clean throughout.

## HTML email template system, with org/platform branding and a one-click unsubscribe link

User request: every outgoing email (instant notifications, the daily digest, the org/system "send test email" actions, the disk-usage alert) was plain text with zero branding. Wanted a real HTML template system per a supplied design brief (clean modern SaaS email, light/dark theme support, self-contained embedded images, one clear CTA, understated footer with legal/company info and an unsubscribe link), with the default header/logo area specifically "the platform icon, the header title on a black background", brandable per organisation the same way the UI already is, falling back to a platform default. Two explicit follow-up requirements: Jinja2 (the templating engine chosen for this) had to be used safely given its real CVE history, and every one of the footer/branding fields had to be overridable per-org, not just platform-wide.

- **Jinja2 pinned to 3.1.6, autoescape always on, and — the actual mitigation — templates are never built from a runtime string.** The user cited a specific CVE number that turned out not to exist in NVD/Snyk/any vendor advisory; rather than chase an unverifiable ID, this was designed against the real, confirmed Jinja2 CVE history instead (2024-22195/34064 `xmlattr`-filter XSS, 2024-56201/56326 sandbox escapes, 2025-27516 sandboxed `|attr` bypass — all fixed by 3.1.6). All four content templates load exclusively from static files on disk via `FileSystemLoader` (`backend/app/templates/email/`); nothing ever calls `Environment.from_string` with org-/user-supplied text, which is what actually rules out the server-side template injection class those sandbox-escape CVEs depend on (they all require the *template source* to be attacker-influenced, not just a variable's value) — so a `SandboxedEnvironment` isn't needed either, there's no path for a caller to supply template code at all. No `|safe`/`Markup(...)` is used on any org-/user-supplied value; the one legitimate near-miss — turning `email_footer_address`'s embedded newlines into `<br>` — is a custom `nl2br` filter (`services/email_templates.py`) that escapes the raw value *first* and only marks the already-escaped, safe result `Markup` afterwards, never the reverse. A dedicated regression test (`test_email_templates.py::test_notification_email_escapes_org_and_notification_supplied_content`) renders every user-controlled field (org header title, footer company name/address, notification title) with `<script>`/`onerror=` payloads and asserts the raw payload never appears unescaped in the output — verified passing before this was considered done.

- **Branding resolution reuses the existing org-overrides-platform-default two-tier shape verbatim, extended to three new fields, per the user's follow-up.** `resolve_email_branding` (`services/email_branding.py`) resolves the header logo/title/accent colour from `Organization.logo_file_id`/`header_title`/`accent_color_hex` falling back per-field to `ServerSettings` — exactly what `BrandingContext.tsx` already does client-side for UI chrome, mirrored server-side since email renders in the backend. Three new fields, `email_footer_company_name`/`email_footer_website`/`email_footer_address`, were added to *both* `Organization` and `ServerSettings` with identical null-means-inherit semantics (not platform-only, which was the first draft — the user corrected this explicitly: an org must be able to set its own footer identity, same path as every other branding field). `email_footer_company_name` falls back to a hardcoded "ReqTrackManager" when unset anywhere, same as `header_title`'s fallback to the built-in product name. Digest and the two platform-level alerts (system test email, disk-usage warning) always resolve with `organization_id=None` — deliberate, since a digest can span notifications from multiple orgs and there's no single "right" org to brand it with.

- **Header banner is a literal HTML block, not a generated raster image.** "The platform icon, header title on a black background" is a full-width `#000000` table row containing the resolved logo (`<img src="cid:brand_logo">`, omitted entirely — not a broken-image icon — when no logo is configured anywhere) and the header title in white, unthemed by design (only the body/footer surfaces switch with `prefers-color-scheme`). This keeps one visually consistent brand mark regardless of theme and avoids a whole raster-generation subsystem (Pillow, already a transitive dependency via `qrcode[pil]`, was available but unnecessary) for something table-based CSS does natively and more email-client-compatibly.

- **Images embedded via `multipart/related` `cid:`, never an external URL**, per the design brief. `services/email.py`'s `send_email`/`send_email_async` gained `html_body`/`inline_images` parameters; when a logo is present, `EmailMessage.add_related()` attaches it under the HTML part (verified structurally with a standalone MIME-tree dump before wiring it into the real send path, then again against real MailHog captures — see Verified below). The logo bytes are fetched via the existing `services/files.py::read_file`/`FileAsset.content_type` — no new upload or storage mechanism.

- **One-click unsubscribe is a stateless signed JWT that flips the exact same field the logged-in Preferences page controls, with every use audited.** Per the user's explicit follow-up ("please do both, one-click unsubscribe, but that updates the login preferences... log when it's been used"): `create_email_unsubscribe_token`/`decode_email_unsubscribe_token` (`security.py`) mirror the existing `purpose`-claim JWT pattern (`create_access_token`/`create_oidc_state_token`), with a 2-year expiry rather than the usual short session-token lifetime — deliberately, since the token grants no session and can only ever set `User.email_digest_mode = DigestMode.NONE` (the identical field `PUT /auth/me` already lets a logged-in user edit), so the usual "keep it short-lived" reasoning for a credential doesn't apply: the worst case of a leaked link is someone else disabling that user's email notifications, an availability/annoyance issue, not a confidentiality or integrity breach. The new public, unauthenticated `GET /notifications/unsubscribe` endpoint (`routers/notifications.py`) is a deliberate, narrow exception to normal authenticated-request handling — consulted against `docs/soc2/policies/access-control-policy.md` and `system-operations-monitoring-and-logging-policy.md` first, per this repo's required identify→verify→remediate step for new unauthenticated surfaces — mirroring the precedent the access-control policy already documents for "shared UI chrome" (the platform logo's own openly-readable endpoint): unauthenticated by necessity (a link clicked from a mail client has no session to present), narrowly scoped (one low-sensitivity, already-user-controlled preference, nothing else reachable), and every use — successful or a rejected/expired/tampered token — writes an `AuditEvent` (`email_unsubscribed_via_link` / `email_unsubscribe_link_rejected`), satisfying the logging requirement explicitly rather than leaving misuse invisible.

- **All five existing send-sites converted, not just new ones added alongside them**, per the user's "all of them" answer: `notify()`'s instant path, the daily digest, the org- and system-scoped test-email actions, and the disk-usage alert (`services/disk_monitor.py`, which gained a `db: Session` parameter to `check_disk_usage_once` purely to resolve platform branding — existing tests updated to pass one). Every plain-text `send_email`/`send_email_async` callsite in the codebase now also passes `html_body`/`inline_images`.

**Verified**: full backend pytest suite — `docker compose exec backend python -m pytest -q` (real MailHog, no mocking of the SMTP transport at the aiosmtplib level for the invite-email tests, which is why this must run inside the container rather than a host venv where the `mailhog` hostname doesn't resolve) — **433 passed**, including 4 new template-rendering tests (`test_email_templates.py`, one of them the XSS-escaping regression above), 8 new unsubscribe-token tests (`test_email_unsubscribe.py` — valid token flips state + audits, idempotent re-use, garbage/expired/wrong-purpose/tampered/unknown-user tokens all rejected without side effects), and extended assertions on the existing `test_digest.py`/`test_org_rename_and_test_email.py` confirming real HTML (not a bare string) is what's actually sent. `ruff check app tests` clean. Frontend: `tsc --noEmit`, `eslint .` (2 pre-existing, already-accepted warnings unrelated to this change), and `npm run build` all clean; the full Storybook interaction suite — **262 passed across all 43 story files** — including new/extended stories for the platform and per-org email-footer fields (`ServerManagementPage.stories.tsx`'s `PlatformBrandingEmailFooter`, `OrgAdminPage.stories.tsx`'s `BrandingSectionEmailFooterSave`). Manual verification against the real rebuilt dev/test Compose stack: a platform-default test email (no org context, no logo configured) rendered the black header/title, default accent-coloured CTA, and "© 2026 ReqTrackManager" footer fallback exactly as designed; a second test email after setting an organisation's own header title, accent colour, logo, and footer company/website/address (with the org's SMTP override pointed at the same MailHog instance) showed all of it correctly overriding the platform default, including a genuine `multipart/related` `image/png` MIME part referenced via `cid:brand_logo` in the header `<img>` tag; a real instant notification (a password-change) was confirmed via direct DB/audit-table inspection to both arrive with the footer's "Manage your email preferences" link and, on visiting that link, flip `email_digest_mode` from `instant` to `none` and write the `email_unsubscribed_via_link` audit row. Full Playwright suite (`npx playwright test`, 45 specs) passed, including 3 in the extended `org-rename-and-test-email.spec.ts` that now decode the actual MIME/quoted-printable HTML body captured by MailHog and assert on its content (dark-mode CSS present, org branding resolved, the unsubscribe link present with a real token) rather than only asserting delivery happened. `backend/scripts/seed_demo_data.py` and `seed_e2e_dataset.py` were both updated to set org-level `email_footer_*` values on the orgs they create (Solstice Robotics and E2E Alpha Robotics respectively) so the new fields are exercised by default rather than left null; platform-wide `ServerSettings.email_footer_*` was deliberately left unset by both scripts, since mutating global deployment settings from a per-org demo/E2E seeder felt like the wrong layer — the platform-default fallback path is already covered by the manual MailHog verification and `test_platform_branding_falls_back_to_built_in_product_name_when_unset`.

## Production incident: `email_footer_*` columns missing after the HTML template system deploy, and a regression test for the whole bug class

**Identify**: after pulling the latest image to the production server, every request touching `ServerSettings` (`GET /api/v1/system/test-email`, and by extension anything calling `resolve_email_branding`) failed with `psycopg2.errors.UndefinedColumn: column server_settings.email_footer_company_name does not exist`. The "HTML email template system" change above (`07570c1`) added `email_footer_company_name`/`email_footer_website`/`email_footer_address` to both `Organization` and `ServerSettings` in `app/models/organization.py`, but no Alembic migration was ever written to add those columns — an oversight that went undetected because the backend test suite's schema fixture and any dev workflow using `Base.metadata.create_all()` both build the schema straight from the current model classes, so they never exercise the gap between "the model has the field" and "a migration adds the column." A real deployment running `alembic upgrade head` (`app/migrations.py`, on every startup) does exercise exactly that gap, which is how this reached production without being caught locally or by the existing suite.

**Verify**: confirmed via `grep` across `backend/alembic/versions/` that no migration referenced `email_footer_*` anywhere, and reproduced the missing columns directly against the `tests/container` Postgres instance (`\d server_settings` / `\d organizations`) after rebuilding and recreating the `backend` container so it actually picked up the new migration file (Compose doesn't bind-mount source — a stale image otherwise silently tests against yesterday's code).

**Remediated**:
1. `backend/alembic/versions/0007_email_footer_branding.py` adds all six columns (`email_footer_company_name`/`_website`/`_address` on both `organizations` and `server_settings`) using the same `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` pattern 0002–0006 already use, for the same reason: convergent on both a fresh `create_all()`'d database and an already-migrated one missing the columns.
2. A new regression test, `backend/tests/test_schema_migrations_match_models.py::test_models_and_migrations_have_not_drifted`, guards against this exact bug class recurring. Since `conftest.py`'s session-scoped `_schema` fixture already builds the test database via real `alembic upgrade head` rather than `create_all()`, the live migrated schema is directly comparable to `Base.metadata` — the test uses `alembic.autogenerate.compare_metadata` (the same diff engine `alembic revision --autogenerate` uses) and fails with the full diff if a model field, table, or index has no matching migration. Running it turned up one genuine pre-existing (harmless) drift unrelated to this incident: `comment_files.comment_id`'s index, created by raw SQL in migration `0004` (`CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS ix_comment_files_comment_id ...`), was never declared on the `CommentFile` model, so autogenerate saw it as an index to drop. Fixed by adding `index=True` to that column (`app/models/file.py`) — SQLAlchemy's default naming convention (`ix_<table>_<column>`) matches the migration's hand-picked name exactly, so this is metadata-only and required no new migration.

**Verified**: `docker compose exec backend python -m alembic upgrade head` inside a freshly rebuilt `tests/container` backend applies `0006`→`0007` cleanly; `\d server_settings`/`\d organizations` confirm all six columns now exist. Full backend pytest suite passed inside the same container, including the new drift test with zero diffs after the `file.py` fix.

## Deployment-wide organisation label override

Requested directly by the user, not derived from `docs/requirements.md` (no requirement ID covers relabelling the "organisation" concept itself — C-C-03's per-project terminology override is a separate, fixed, closed set of nouns that deliberately excludes it, per `docs/decisions.md`'s existing terminology entry). Flagged here per this project's governance the same way the bundle export/import work above was.

**Scope, confirmed with the user before building:** a single deployment-wide setting (`ServerSettings.org_label_singular`/`org_label_plural`), not a per-organisation override — there's no single right answer to "whose branding" the word choice should follow the way accent colour/logo/header title already do, since the word is used to refer to organisations in general, not any one of them. And, per explicit follow-up feedback after an initial narrower draft: **every** string literal containing "organisation"/"Organisation(s)" in `frontend/src/i18n/strings.ts` was converted, not just the primary chrome (nav/page titles/column headers) originally proposed — full-sentence hints/confirmations included. Two exceptions, both deliberate: the platform-branding admin form's own "Organisation label (singular/plural)" field labels stay in English always (they describe the control, not content subject to it), and `ENTITY_TYPE_LABEL`'s `"organization"` entry in `api/types.ts` was replaced by an explicit branch in `activityEntityLabel` (it's a plain module-level `Record`, which can't call the `useOrgLabel` hook).

**Mechanism**: each affected `strings.ts` entry changed from a plain string to a function of the resolved label(s) (e.g. `notProvisioned: (org: string) => \`Your ${org} has not provisioned you access.\``), called at each site with `orgLabel`/`orgLabelPlural`/`orgLabelCapitalized` from three new hooks in `frontend/src/context/BrandingContext.tsx` (mirroring `useTerm`/`useTermPlural`'s existing shape for C-C-03).
