This type change is also the completeness mechanism: every call site still treating the value as a plain string (e.g. `{strings.auth.notProvisioned}` in JSX) became a `tsc` compile error, so the sweep across ~30 files was verified by the type checker rather than by grep/manual review alone — a plain `grep -rn "organisation"` pass afterward still caught a handful of genuinely-hardcoded literals `tsc` couldn't (a few raw JSX strings in `OrgAdminPage.tsx`/`ServerOrganisationsPage.tsx` that had never gone through `strings.ts` at all), which were fixed the same way.

**Backend**: two nullable `String(50)` columns on `ServerSettings`, `NULL` meaning "use the built-in English word" — same convention as `default_header_title`. Migration `0006_org_label.py` (see the incident entry above for why a migration is never optional here). Threaded through the existing `GET`/`PUT /system/branding` endpoint and `ServerSettingsOut`/`ServerSettingsUpdate` schemas rather than a new endpoint, since it's platform branding.

**Verified**: `backend/tests/test_org_export_import.py` and the wider suite pass unaffected; `tsc -b --noEmit` clean across the frontend after the full conversion; the Storybook interaction suite's new `PlatformBrandingOrgLabel` story (`ServerManagementPage.stories.tsx`) confirms the two new fields round-trip through `PUT /system/branding`; a new Playwright spec (`org-label.spec.ts`) sets a custom label via the real admin UI, confirms the nav rail and server-organisations page pick it up, and restores the default afterward (`test.afterAll`, the same global-setting reset pattern `self-signup.spec.ts` already uses for `ServerSettings.signup_mode`). `eslint .`: 0 errors; the three new hooks add three more `react-refresh/only-export-components` warnings to `BrandingContext.tsx` (same rule/file this repo's own "HTML email template system" verification note already accepted 1 pre-existing instance of, for `useBranding` — exporting a Provider component alongside its consumer hooks from one file is standard React context practice, not something worth restructuring around for this rule).

## Merge-import into an existing organisation

Requested directly by the user alongside the organisation-label work above, with two explicit scoping decisions made during review before implementation:

1. **No "pick two existing orgs and merge them" UI.** This is the existing org bundle export/import mechanism (`services.org_export`, see the "Bundle export/import" entry above), pointed at an organisation the caller already administers instead of always creating a brand-new one — an org admin exports (or receives) a bundle and imports it into their own existing organisation.
2. **Scoped to users, groups, projects, and report templates only** — the target organisation's own profile (branding, SMTP, SSO/OIDC config, logo, login background, default template project, `slug`, `is_active`, `pat_max_lifetime_days`) is never read from the bundle or written to. This was narrowed from an initial draft that also planned to let org-level settings be merged with a keep/override choice per field; the user cut that scope explicitly, which also removes most of the conflict surface a "some data already exists" merge would otherwise need to reason about.

**Security decisions** (identify → verify → remediate, per the change-management policy — same standard the original bundle work and its hardening pass above were held to, since this crosses an *existing* tenant boundary in a way the original "always brand-new organisation" design explicitly never had to):

- **Code reuse over duplication, per explicit review feedback.** `services.org_export` was refactored so `import_org_bundle` (unchanged behaviour, brand-new org) and the new `merge_org_bundle` (existing org) share the same parsing/import helpers — `_parse_org_bundle`, `_import_members`, `_import_org_groups`, `_import_report_templates`, `_import_projects` — parameterised by whether conflicts are possible (`resolutions`/`merge_by_name`), rather than a second near-identical implementation.
- **A project is never overwritten in place on conflict** — only skipped or imported as a renamed copy (`"<name> (imported)"`). A project carries real, potentially large requirement/change-request history; silently replacing that is a materially bigger and more destructive feature than this one, and wasn't asked for. A report template has no such history, so its conflict resolution *does* allow an in-place overwrite (`"use_import"`) — the asymmetry is deliberate, not an oversight.
- **Users and org groups are always additive, never a conflict.** An existing member keeps their current role (a bundle member who already holds it is a silent no-op, not a duplicate-row error); an existing group gains the bundle's members as a union, never loses one. Neither can be downgraded or removed by a merge, closing off a privilege-de-escalation-via-import surface before it existed.
- **A real bug found during test-writing, not just reasoned about**: `_import_members`'s "attribute the project-manager fallback to the first matched `org_admin` member, not the importer" logic (the same fix recorded in the original bundle-import bug entry above) is correct for a brand-new org with no established admin yet, but wrong for a merge — the *target* org's own admin (`current_user`, performing the merge) is already the obviously legitimate fallback, and reassigning it to some *other* organisation's admin who merely happens to also be a bundle member would be a regression, not a fix. Caught by `test_merge_with_no_conflicts_adds_users_groups_projects_and_templates` failing to find the merged project via the target admin's own project list. Remediated with a new `reassign_acting_user` flag on `_import_members`: `True` for `import_org_bundle`, `False` for `merge_org_bundle`.
- **Authorization**: `require_org_role(ORG_ADMIN)` on the target organisation for both `POST /orgs/{id}/import/preview` and `.../import/merge` — the same bar `GET /orgs/{id}/export` already uses, deliberately no server-admin bypass (I-M-05).
- **`resolutions` must cover every conflict exactly, or the whole import is refused (400)** rather than silently defaulting an unresolved conflict either way — `merge_org_bundle` recomputes the same conflict set the preview endpoint reported and diffs it against the supplied keys before writing anything. An invalid resolution value for a conflict's kind (e.g. `"keep_existing"` on a project conflict) is rejected the same way.

**New endpoints/schemas**: `POST /orgs/{id}/import/preview` (`OrgMergePreviewResult`/`MergeConflictOut`) and `POST /orgs/{id}/import/merge` (`OrgMergeResult`) in `routers/orgs.py`; `detect_merge_conflicts`/`merge_org_bundle` in `services/org_export.py`. Frontend: `ImportConflictPanel.tsx` (grouped by conflict kind, radio choice per item, defaulting to the non-destructive option — skip/keep-existing — so submitting without touching every choice can never surprise-overwrite or surprise-duplicate anything), wired into a new collapsible section on `OrgAdminPage.tsx`.

## Hardening pass on org merge-import and rename

Requested follow-up ("do a hardening pass") to the previous two entries (organisation-label override and merge-import). Identify → verify → remediate, per the change-management policy's practice for security-sensitive changes; scoped to the most recent commit's actual surface (`44ba1bf`: merge-import, org rename, deployment-wide org label, and the two backfill migrations).

**Found and fixed: a bundle-driven import could hand a banned account real access back, bypassing `User.is_banned` entirely.** That invariant is already enforced at two call sites — `assign_org_role` and `assign_project_role_by_email` (see the "Hardening pass on self-signup/invites/external-users" entry above) — specifically so a known-bad account flagged by a server admin "can't quietly be let back in through a different org's admin... without a server admin needing to intervene again per org" (`User.is_banned`'s own docstring). `_import_members` (shared by `import_org_bundle` and the new `merge_org_bundle`) matched a bundle member by email and granted `UserOrgRole` directly with no `is_banned` check at all — a third, unguarded path to the exact same grant. Worse, `_import_org_groups` had the same gap on a second, independent axis: `rbac.get_effective_project_roles` resolves project access through `OrgGroupMember` rows with no separate check that the user holds any `UserOrgRole` at all (an org group nested into a `ProjectGroup` grants access purely by group membership), so a banned account added to an org group via a bundle would gain real project access without ever touching `UserOrgRole` or being caught by the fix above on its own.

This gap technically predates this commit — `_import_members`'s logic (minus the `merge_org_bundle`-specific `reassign_acting_user` behaviour) is a straight refactor of `import_org_bundle`'s pre-existing member-import loop, confirmed by diffing against the parent commit — but `import_org_bundle` is `require_server_admin`-only, a small, highly-trusted population. `merge_org_bundle` exposes the same unguarded logic to *any* `org_admin` of *any* organisation in the deployment, and does so against an org's real, live membership rather than a freshly-created empty one — a materially larger and more realistic exploit surface than the pre-existing gap on its own, so this is treated as a regression this commit introduced, not merely an old gap left as-is. The realistic shape isn't even adversarial: export an org for backup/migration, have one of its members banned afterward, then merge that now-stale bundle back in later — without this fix, the ban would be silently undone.

Fixed by skipping (with a warning, same style as the existing "member has no account — invited instead" warning) an `is_banned` match in both `_import_members` and `_import_org_groups`, threaded through both `import_org_bundle` and `merge_org_bundle` alike — closing the gap for both paths, not just the newly-exposed one. New test: `test_merge_does_not_grant_a_role_or_group_membership_to_a_banned_member`, which captures a bundle with a real member + group membership, then has that account leave and get banned *after* export (the only order `ban`/`add_org_group_member`'s own membership preconditions allow), and asserts the merge into a fresh target org grants neither the org role nor the group membership, surfacing a warning instead.

**Reviewed and found adequate, no changes needed**: `rename_organization`'s authorization (`ORG_ADMIN`, no server-admin bypass, matching every other org-property endpoint) and validation (reuses `OrganizationCreate`'s own non-blank-name validator). `preview_organization_merge`/`merge_organization_bundle`'s upload handling (`_parse_org_bundle` already applies `enforce_upload_size_limit`/`enforce_zip_uncompressed_size_limit`, the same zip-bomb guards the original bundle-import hardening pass added) and `resolutions` parsing (malformed JSON and non-string values are both rejected with 400 before any write). `merge_org_bundle`'s "any org_admin can point at any bundle, including one exported from an org they have no relationship to" shape — confirmed this is the feature as scoped ("an org admin exports (or receives) a bundle and imports it into their own existing organisation"), not an unreviewed cross-tenant hole: it can only ever grant what the *target* org's own admin could already grant by hand (project creation, member invites via `assign_org_role`), gated behind that same `ORG_ADMIN` bar, and the additive-only/no-overwrite design (see the entry above) means it can't destroy the target's own data either. `org_group`/`report_template`/project name-collision detection being case-insensitive-by-`.lower()`-only (no Unicode normalisation) — consistent with every other name-uniqueness check already in this codebase (stages/components/categories), not a new relaxation. `0006_org_label.py`/`0007_email_footer_branding.py` migrations — columns match `ServerSettings`/`Organization` model fields exactly, `IF NOT EXISTS` pattern consistent with 0002-0005, and the new `test_schema_migrations_match_models.py` (added in the same commit) now pins the whole bug class the second migration was itself written to fix. `org_label_singular`/`org_label_plural` and `email_footer_*` rendering — used in ~40 frontend call sites, all plain JSX text interpolation (no `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` anywhere in the codebase), so React's default escaping applies; email rendering already has its own escaping test (`test_notification_email_escapes_org_and_notification_supplied_content`). The merge-preview/confirm frontend flow (`OrgAdminPage.tsx`) reuses the same in-memory `File` object for both calls rather than letting the user swap it between preview and confirm — moot as a server-side control regardless, since `merge_org_bundle` always recomputes conflicts from the bytes actually posted to `/import/merge`, never trusts the preview call's result.

**Follow-up ("look deeper"): traced invariants beyond the endpoint-level review above**, the same way the "Deeper hardening pass" entry earlier in this log followed its own first pass — looking at what the new code depends on and interacts with, not just re-reading the same lines.

**Found and fixed: `merge_org_bundle`'s audit event recorded *that* a merge happened but not *where the data came from*.** The system-operations-monitoring-and-logging-policy's first rule requires audit entries to record "who did it, when, and what changed — not reconstructed after the fact." `merged_from_bundle`'s `detail` had `actor_id` (who) and per-entity-kind counts (what), but nothing identifying which organisation's bundle was merged in, who exported it, or when — despite that being right there in the bundle's own `manifest.json` and already being parsed into a local variable (`_manifest`, underscore-prefixed specifically because it went unused). For most bundle-import paths in this codebase this is a pre-existing, lower-stakes gap (`import_org_bundle`, and `project_export.py`'s equivalent, both create a brand-new entity from scratch, so "what changed" is self-evident from the new org/project's own audit trail) — left as-is here rather than expanded into an unrelated refactor. `merge_org_bundle` is different: it's the one path in the whole bundle subsystem that writes into an organisation's *real, live* data, which is exactly why it was already held to a stricter authorization/isolation bar than the others in the first entry above — the same reasoning extends to its audit trail. Without the source recorded, "a project neither of us remembers creating just appeared in our org" has no way to be answered from the audit log alone. Fixed by adding `source_org_name`/`source_exported_by_email`/`source_exported_at` (the manifest's own self-reported fields — same trust level as the rest of the bundle's contents, not independently re-verified) to the log event's `detail`. New test: `test_merge_audit_event_records_the_source_bundles_provenance`, which queries `AuditEvent` directly (the established pattern from `test_email_unsubscribe.py`) and asserts all three fields are populated after a real merge.

**Reviewed and found adequate, no changes needed, on the deeper pass**: project-name uniqueness is not a database constraint anywhere in this codebase (`Project.name` has no `unique=True`/`UniqueConstraint`), so a TOCTOU race between two concurrent merges (or a manual create and a merge) producing two same-named projects is pre-existing accepted behaviour, not a new hole this feature opens. File/attachment scoping under merge (`apply_project_data` derives `organization_id` from the newly-created project's own row — the merge *target's* id — never from the bundle's own data), confirmed by reading `project_export.py` directly rather than assuming the existing project-bundle hardening still holds after this reuse. A banned user already holding the exact role/group membership `_import_members`/`_import_org_groups` would otherwise warn about skipping is not currently reachable — `ban_orphaned_user` only ever bans an account with zero org roles anywhere, and (post the fix above) every remaining grant path checks `is_banned` — so the warning's phrasing was left as unconditional rather than adding dead-code-guarding complexity for a state the invariant itself rules out.

**Verified for real**: new regression test passes in isolation and as part of the full suite; full backend suite — 443 passed, zero regressions, run against the real `tests/container` Postgres stack. `ruff check` clean on both touched files.

**Verified**: `backend/tests/test_org_import_merge.py` — 8 new tests (no-conflict merge; a project-name collision resolved both ways; a report-template-name collision resolved both ways; the target org's own profile fields provably unchanged after a merge; missing/invalid `resolutions` both rejected; a non-admin-of-the-target caller rejected; a wrong-kind bundle rejected) — plus the full pre-existing `test_org_export_import.py` suite confirming the refactor didn't change `import_org_bundle`'s own behaviour. `ruff check` clean. Frontend: `tsc -b --noEmit` clean; the Storybook interaction suite's new `ImportConflictPanel.stories.tsx` (5 tests) and the existing `OrgAdminPage.stories.tsx` (15 tests, unaffected by the new section) both pass. A new Playwright spec (`org-merge-import.spec.ts`) drives the real UI end to end: exports a source organisation with a project, uploads it into a disposable target organisation that already has a same-named project, resolves the conflict as "import as a new copy" through the actual conflict panel, and confirms both the resulting two projects and the target's untouched branding via the API afterward.

## User-reported bug: login-page background image "didn't persist"

User reported that a background image set for the "Server Management Platform" login page didn't persist. Identify → verify → remediate, per the change-management policy's practice for security-sensitive changes (this touches unauthenticated access to a file-download endpoint).

**Identified**: the image did persist — the bug was that it never rendered on the actual login page. Two stacked access-control gaps, both pre-existing:
1. `GET /api/v1/system/branding` (`routers/system.py`) required `get_current_user`, but `LoginPage.tsx` calls it on mount, before any session exists, to fetch `default_login_background_file_id`. The request 401'd, the `.then()` never ran, and `loginBackgroundFileId` state stayed `null` — so the plain `/login` page's `useEffect` fetch silently failed and no background was ever requested.
2. Even with (1) fixed, `GET /api/v1/files/{file_id}` (`routers/files.py::download_file`) unconditionally required a bearer token via `get_current_user_header_or_query`. This meant no anonymous visitor could load *any* login-page image, including the org logo already shown on the org-branded `/login/{slug}` page (`OrgLoginPage.tsx`) — an equally real instance of the same root cause, just not the one reported.

Confirmed `ServerSettingsOut` (the branding schema) carries no sensitive fields (no SMTP credentials, no OIDC secrets), so making it fully public mirrors the existing precedent of `orgs.py::get_org_login_info`, which already serves org branding (including `login_background_file_id`) with no authentication at all.

**Remediated**: `get_branding` dropped its `current_user` dependency entirely (now unauthenticated, matching `get_org_login_info`). `deps.py` gained `get_current_user_header_or_query_optional`, identical to `get_current_user_header_or_query` except it returns `None` instead of raising when no token is presented at all (a token that *is* present but invalid/expired still 401s as before — only the "no token" case is relaxed, so a broken session doesn't silently downgrade to anonymous). `download_file` now resolves the current user through that optional dependency and checks a `is_public_branding` bucket — `Organization.logo_file_id`, `Organization.login_background_file_id`, `ServerSettings.default_logo_file_id`, `ServerSettings.default_login_background_file_id` — before any auth check at all; only avatars, org shared resources, and requirement/comment attachments still require `current_user is not None`, unchanged from before. Avatars deliberately stayed in the "authenticated but not anonymous" bucket — there's no pre-login surface that renders one, unlike logos/login backgrounds.

**Verified**: five new backend tests in `test_files.py` — anonymous download succeeds for org logo, org login background, platform default logo, and platform default login background (all four independently, both upload sources); `GET /api/v1/system/branding` succeeds with no `Authorization` header; and a regression test confirms avatars and org shared resources still 401 anonymously, pinning the boundary of the carve-out. Full backend suite run inside the real `tests/container` stack (per this project's established Docker-test workflow, since `backend/tests/`'s `DATABASE_URL` guard requires it): 448 passed, 0 failed, 0 regressions. `docker compose restart backend` + both seed scripts re-run afterward to restore the dev/e2e data the suite run wipes.

## Groups/permissions overhaul, part 1: project visibility ("Only specified" vs "Org-wide visibility")

User request: a large groups/permissions overhaul (true org-group nesting, project visibility, OAuth/SAML group sync, unified user-management UI — see the four-feature plan discussed and approved in-session). Per the user's explicit direction, none of this is added to `docs/requirements.md` (which the agent may not edit) — it's recorded here in the decisions log instead, following this project's identify → verify → remediate practice for the parts that touch authorization. This entry covers part 1 only: project visibility. The user also explicitly descoped an earlier idea (a new org-wide "project admin of all projects" role) in favour of this simpler visibility toggle.

**What was built**: a `Project.visibility` field (`ProjectVisibility`: `only_specified` default, or `org_wide`). The grant is implemented inside `services.rbac.get_effective_project_roles` itself, not just `list_projects`'s bespoke accessible-id union — every `require_project_*` dependency funnels through that one function, so an org-wide project's content (not just its list-page entry) becomes visible uniformly. When `visibility == org_wide` and the caller holds any role at all in the project's own organisation, they get baseline `ProjectRole.MEMBER` — never manager/administrator/stakeholder, which still require an explicit assignment. Deliberately **not** folded into `get_project_member_user_ids` (broadcast notification targeting, C-N-01) — every org member getting notified for every org-wide project would be a worse regression than the gap it closes — nor into `can_manage_project_settings`, which still requires a real role or org-admin.

The toggle is exposed in two places: the "New project" creation form (`ProjectListPage.tsx`, always explicit — never inherited from a cloned template, so a template happening to be org-wide doesn't silently make every project cloned from it org-wide too) and `ProjectAdminPage.tsx`'s settings tab (existing `PATCH /projects/{id}`, audit-logged via the established `log_event` pattern).

**Verified**: four new backend tests in `test_rbac.py` (baseline MEMBER access granted with no explicit role, scoped strictly to the project's own organisation, never implies manage rights, and the visibility change is itself audit-logged) plus `test_schema_migrations_match_models.py` confirming the new migration (`0008_project_visibility.py`) matches the model. Full backend suite: 452 passed (448 + 4 new), 0 regressions, run inside the real `tests/container` stack. `ruff check` clean. Frontend: `tsc -b`, `eslint .` clean (same 5 pre-existing, already-accepted warnings); new Storybook story (`OverviewTabSetOrgWideVisibility`) and a new Playwright spec (`project-visibility.spec.ts`) exercising the real UI end to end — org-wide visibility set at creation, confirmed on the settings tab, a plain org member with zero explicit role granted access, then the toggle reversed and access revoked — using a disposable project created within the spec specifically so it can't interfere with `project-access-scope.spec.ts`'s existing assertion that a scoped member does *not* see other org projects by default.

One real regression caught by re-running the broader Playwright suite (not just the new spec): the "New project" form's new, unconditional Visibility `<select>` broke two existing tests that had encoded "no select elements when there's only one org to choose from" — their actual intent (per their own comments) was "no *org-picker* select," which happened to coincide with "no selects at all" only because the org picker and the template picker were previously the form's only conditional `<select>`s. Fixed `project-list-org-scoping.spec.ts`'s assertion to expect exactly the Visibility select (not zero), and `project-bundle-export-import.spec.ts`'s one-shot `isVisible()` check on `select.first()` — which could now latch onto the always-present Visibility select before the org picker had asynchronously mounted — with the same retrying wait-for-content pattern `multi-org-admin.spec.ts` already used for the identical race. Also cleaned up disposable orgs left behind by my own earlier failed local runs of `project-list-org-scoping.spec.ts` (it deletes its own scaffolding at the end, but only on the success path) before re-verifying.

## Groups/permissions overhaul, part 2: true org-group nesting

Continuation of the plan from part 1 above. An `OrgGroup` can now contain another `OrgGroup` as a member, transitively — a distinct, new relationship from the existing (and deliberately one-level-deep) org-group-in-*project*-group nesting (C-U-12).

**What was built**: `OrgGroupMember` gained a nullable, self-referential `member_org_group_id` FK (mirroring `ProjectGroupMember`'s existing user-or-org-group XOR shape one level up); `user_id` became nullable with a check constraint enforcing exactly one of the two is set. Cycle prevention (`would_create_org_group_cycle`, `services/rbac.py`) runs at write time in `add_org_group_member` — rejects self-nesting and any nesting that would close a longer loop. Transitive resolution uses two new iterative BFS closure helpers (`_ancestor_org_group_ids`/`_descendant_org_group_ids`) rather than a recursive SQL CTE, matching this file's existing preference for explicit Python-side set operations over SQL recursion; wired into `get_effective_project_roles` (a user in a sub-group now counts as a member of every ancestor group for project-role resolution) and `get_project_member_user_ids` (broadcast notifications now reach members of descendant groups too). A defensive iteration cap exists but should never trip, since cycles are already rejected at write time.

Found and fixed along the way, before it could ship as a silent gap: org bundle export/import (`services/org_export.py`) would have dropped nested-group structure entirely — `OrgGroupMember.user_id`-keyed queries naturally excluded the new nested-group rows (NULL never matches a join), so an exported bundle simply never carried `nested_group_names`. Fixed by adding that field to the export JSON and a second import pass (after every group in the bundle is created/resolved, since a group can reference another one appearing later in the same list) that wires up nesting edges by name — with the same cycle check and warn-and-skip behavior as the live endpoint, for a malformed or adversarial bundle.

**Verified**: `backend/tests/test_org_group_nesting.py` (new, 7 tests) — self-nesting rejected, a longer 2-hop cycle rejected, a 3-hop cycle rejected, cross-org nesting rejected, transitive resolution through a 3-level chain actually grants the project role, a nested-group member can be removed via the generic `member_id` delete endpoint, and `leave_organization`'s cleanup is unaffected by nesting edges being present. Full backend suite: 459 passed (452 + 7 new), 0 regressions, including `test_schema_migrations_match_models.py` (migration `0009_org_group_nesting.py` matches the model) and the full pre-existing `test_org_export_import.py`/`test_org_import_merge.py` suites (confirming the nested-group export/import addition didn't change existing bundle behaviour). `ruff check` clean. Frontend: `tsc -b`, `eslint .` clean (same 5 pre-existing warnings); `OrgAdminPage.tsx`'s groups section gained a nest/un-nest picker with cycle-rejection error surfaced inline; new Storybook story (`GroupsSectionNestGroup`) plus a fix to the pre-existing `GroupsSectionShowsMembers` story (a second group fixture, needed to exercise nesting, made its `getByText("Engineering")` query ambiguous against the new nesting `<option>` — scoped to the `<span>` specifically). New Playwright spec (`org-group-nesting.spec.ts`) drives the real UI: create two groups, nest one inside the other, confirm it's listed, attempt the reverse nesting and confirm the cycle error surfaces inline rather than failing silently, then remove the nested-group edge and confirm it's gone.

## Groups/permissions overhaul, part 3: IdP group-membership sync (OIDC extension + SCIM, replacing the originally-planned SAML)

Continuation of the plan from parts 1-2 above. Original scope was extending OIDC group-claims sync to `OrgGroup` membership, plus adding SAML as a second SSO login protocol. The user redirected mid-build on the second half: SAML only carries group information as a side effect of an active login (can't promptly deprovision someone who stops logging in), whereas SCIM is the purpose-built push-provisioning protocol for exactly this job (the IdP calls into this app whenever group membership changes, no login required) and is what Okta/Entra ID/Google Workspace actually use. SAML was dropped from scope entirely; SCIM replaced it.

**Part A — OIDC extension**: `OrgGroup.idp_synced_group_name` (nullable, unique per org via a partial index) marks a group as IdP-managed. New `services/oidc_provisioning.py::sync_org_groups_from_claims`, structurally identical to the existing `sync_org_roles_from_claims` — same vocabulary-scoping (only ever touches groups with the field set) and "no claim at all is unknown, not empty" rule — called alongside it in `auth_oidc.py::oidc_callback`. Configured per group via a new `PATCH /orgs/{id}/groups/{id}` endpoint and an inline input in `OrgAdminPage.tsx`'s groups section.

**Part B — SCIM 2.0 provisioning** (`backend/app/routers/scim.py`): inbound Users/Groups CRUD + group-membership PATCH, authenticated by a per-org bearer token (`Organization.scim_token_hash`/`scim_token_prefix`, hashed the same way as a Personal Access Token — `security.generate_scim_token`/`hash_pat`). A SCIM Group's `id` is this app's own `OrgGroup.id` and a SCIM User's `id` is this app's own `User.id` (no separate external-id mapping table needed — the IdP addresses whatever `id` a `POST` returns). `POST /Users` finds-or-creates by email and grants baseline `OrgRole.MEMBER`; deactivation never hard-deletes (C-U-04/C-U-05); a banned account can never be (re-)provisioned. Deliberately a pragmatic RFC 7643/7644 subset (single `attr eq "value"` filters, `active`/`displayName`/`members` PATCH paths only, no Bulk/ETags/schema extensions) — documented in the router's own module docstring and `docs/enterprise-integration.md`, not hidden. Both sync paths only ever touch `OrgGroupMember` rows with `user_id` set — never `member_org_group_id` (nested-group) edges, which stay structural/admin-managed.

Token management: `GET`/`POST`/`DELETE /orgs/{id}/scim-token` (org-admin only), surfaced in `OrgAdminPage.tsx`'s new "SCIM provisioning" section — the raw token is shown exactly once, at generation.

**A real regression found and fixed via the full Playwright suite, not the new spec itself**: the new per-group IdP-sync "Save" button used the codebase's generic `strings.common.save` ("Save") — inconsistent with this app's own established convention of specific button labels ("Save settings", "Save SSO settings", "Save branding") — which collided with a pre-existing, already-fragile *unscoped* `page.getByRole("button", { name: "Save", exact: true })` in `project-admin-templates-and-reports.spec.ts` (that spec's own comment already documented scoping most of its locators to `reportTemplatesSection` specifically because other sections can be left expanded server-side by another spec sharing the same Gamma persona — this one line had been missed). Whenever `org-group-nesting.spec.ts` (or any other spec) left the "Organisation groups" section expanded for that persona, each existing group's own "Save" button padded out the page-wide match to 3, breaking the strict-mode-sensitive locator. Fixed by giving the new button a specific label (`strings.orgAdmin.saveIdpSync`, "Save sync name") and scoping the pre-existing spec's line to `reportTemplatesSection` too, matching its own already-stated rationale.

**Verified**: `backend/tests/test_oidc_provisioning.py` — 5 new `sync_org_groups_from_claims` tests (grant, no-match, revoke-on-claim-disappearance, never touches an unsynced group or nested-group edges, no-op on a missing claim) plus 2 new endpoint tests (IdP-sync-target round trip via `PATCH`, two groups can't claim the same synced name). `backend/tests/test_scim.py` (new, 11 tests) — token lifecycle (generate/status/revoke, real bearer-auth request), unauthenticated/invalid-token rejection, user provisioning + idempotent re-provisioning + baseline role grant, PATCH deactivation, group create + PATCH add/remove membership (both list-value and single-member-filter path forms), PUT full-replace membership, group delete, cross-org isolation (404 on another org's resources), nested-group edges survive a membership PATCH untouched, and a banned account can't be (re-)provisioned. Full backend suite: 476 passed (459 + 17 new), 0 regressions, including `test_schema_migrations_match_models.py` (migrations `0010_oidc_group_sync.py`/`0011_scim_provisioning.py`, including the partial unique index, match the models exactly). `ruff check` clean. Frontend: `tsc -b`, `eslint .` clean (same 5 pre-existing warnings); new Storybook stories (`GroupsSectionNestGroup`'s sibling `ScimSectionGenerateToken`); new Playwright spec `scim-provisioning.spec.ts` (generates a token through the real UI, uses it for a real authenticated `GET /scim/v2/Users` request proving the whole loop works end to end — not just the UI round-trip — then revokes it and confirms the same token immediately 401s). Full 46-spec Playwright suite run three times against genuinely fresh reseeds while chasing the regression above and two pre-existing flakes (`requirements-and-cr-filters.spec.ts`, already documented elsewhere in this log as a full-batch-only flake; `project-admin-templates-and-reports.spec.ts`, the actual regression above) — final clean run: 46 passed, 0 failed.

## Groups/permissions overhaul, part 4: unified user-management surfaces

Final part of the plan from parts 1-3 above. Closes the original UX gap the user first reported ("org groups can be added as users to projects") plus the "unified user management" ask — extending existing pages rather than building a new monolithic one, per the plan's IA recommendation.

**Closed the original gap**: `ProjectAdminPage.tsx`'s groups tab gained an org-group picker (add/remove) next to the existing per-user `UserAutocomplete` — the backend (`add_project_group_member`) already fully supported `org_group_id`, but no frontend surface ever sent it; only `OrgAdminPage`'s "expanded project" panel could. `member_org_group_ids` is now rendered as an actual list with remove buttons instead of a bare count.

**New self-service `GET /auth/me/memberships`** (`routers/auth.py`): a user's full cross-org picture — org roles, org-group membership (direct and inherited via nesting, reusing the new `services.rbac.get_user_org_group_ids`), and per-project roles — scoped to orgs with a direct `UserOrgRole` (every project-accessible user already has one, C-U-02). Wired into `PreferencesPage.tsx`'s pre-existing "Your access" tab (added in an earlier pass, see that entry above) as a new "Groups" list per org, alongside the org roles/projects it already showed.

**Server-admin access-review directory** (`GET /system/users`) gained a `group_names` field — every org group a user effectively belongs to (direct + inherited) across every org, gated by the same `access_review_show_org_names` setting that already gates `organization_names`, for the identical reason (naming a specific team is at least as revealing of org-internal structure as an org name). Surfaced as a new "Groups" column in `ServerManagementPage.tsx`'s access-review table.

**Considered and explicitly reverted: a new org-admin-scoped nav-rail entry.** The plan called for one, but implementing it surfaced a documented, deliberate prior decision (see this log's "Preferences page: tabbed, plus a new 'Your access' tab" entry): the team had already removed `/orgs`'s own nav-rail link entirely, specifically *because* non-admin/org-admin reachability was moved to the Preferences "Your access" tab's per-org "Manage organisation" link instead of a persistent rail entry. Re-adding a rail entry for the same purpose would have quietly re-introduced the pattern that decision rejected. Reverted the nav-rail change (state, effect, JSX, icon import, strings, and the story exercising it) before it shipped; the "Your access" tab — now itself enhanced with group info by this same pass — already satisfies the underlying "can an org admin easily reach the org(s) they administer" goal.

**Verified**: `backend/tests/test_my_memberships.py` (new, 3 tests: org role + group + project role all present, transitively-inherited groups correctly marked `direct: false`, orgs the caller doesn't belong to are excluded) and one new test in `test_access_review.py` (`group_names` includes both direct and inherited membership). Full backend suite: 480 passed (476 + 4 new), 0 regressions. `ruff check` clean. Frontend: `tsc -b`, `eslint .` clean (same 5 pre-existing warnings); new/extended Storybook coverage (`ProjectAdminPage.stories.tsx`'s `GroupsTabAddOrgGroup` — including a genuine mock-path collision fix, since `/orgs/{id}/groups` also matched the pre-existing `/groups` substring check meant for project groups; `PreferencesPage.stories.tsx`'s `AccessTabShowsOrgsAndProjects` extended for direct/inherited groups; `ServerManagementPage.stories.tsx`'s new `AccessReviewShowsGroups`). New/extended Playwright coverage: a new step in `project-admin-groups-and-fields.spec.ts` (create an org group via API, reload so `ProjectAdminPage`'s once-on-mount org-groups fetch picks it up, nest it into a project group through the real picker, then remove it) and a new step in `preferences-and-theme.spec.ts` (a 3-level-deep nested-group setup via API, confirming the "Your access" tab shows both the direct and the inherited group with the "(via a nested group)" qualifier). Full 46-spec Playwright suite run against a genuinely fresh reseed: 46 passed, 0 failed — including both of the above specs, which had intermittently failed earlier in this pass due to accumulated state pollution (duplicate custom fields, a changed persona password) from dozens of manual re-runs against the same long-lived stack during development, not any product defect; each was independently reproduced as passing cleanly on fresh data before being counted as done.

This closes out the full four-part groups/permissions/visibility/provisioning overhaul from this session (see the "Groups/permissions overhaul" entries above, parts 1-4).

## Hardening pass on the groups/permissions overhaul

Requested follow-up ("please do a hardening pass") to the four parts above. Identify → verify → remediate, per the change-management policy's practice for security-sensitive changes; scoped to that overhaul's actual surface — project visibility, org-group nesting, OIDC/SCIM group sync, and the new unified user-management surfaces.

**Found and fixed: SCIM group deletion cascaded away project access with no record of what changed.** `DELETE /scim/v2/Groups/{id}` (`routers/scim.py::delete_group`) is the *only* org-group-deletion path anywhere in the app — no equivalent exists for a human org admin. `ProjectGroupMember.org_group_id` and `OrgGroupMember.member_org_group_id` both carry `ondelete="CASCADE"` back to `org_groups.id`, so deleting a group silently revokes whatever project access it granted through nesting into a `ProjectGroup`, and detaches it from any parent org group it was nested inside — an IdP routinely deleting/recreating one of its groups can do this with nobody in the app aware anything beyond "a group was deleted" happened. The audit event only ever recorded the group's own id/name. This is the same "record what changed, not just that something happened" gap the merge-import audit-provenance hardening pass (see above) fixed for `merge_org_bundle`'s `detail`, on a different endpoint. Fixed by capturing the affected `ProjectGroup` ids and parent-`OrgGroup` ids *before* the cascade runs and recording both in the `scim_deleted` event's `detail`, so "a project's access just changed and nobody knows why" is answerable from the audit log alone, without changing the deletion behaviour itself (blocking it would break legitimate SCIM deprovisioning). New test: `test_scim_delete_group_audit_event_records_the_access_it_cascaded_away`, which nests a SCIM-managed group both into a project group and inside a parent org group, deletes it, and asserts both ids land in the audit event's detail.

**Found and fixed: a defense-in-depth gap in the new group-membership read path, mirroring a pattern already applied elsewhere.** `services.rbac.get_effective_project_roles`'s existing org-group-in-project-group resolution re-joins on `OrgGroup.organization_id == Project.organization_id` as defense in depth, explicitly commented as such, even though `routers.orgs.add_org_group_member` already rejects cross-org nesting at write time — belt and braces, not redundancy for its own sake. The new `get_user_org_group_ids` (backing `GET /auth/me/memberships` and the access-review directory's `group_names`) computes its transitive ancestor closure via `_ancestor_org_group_ids` with no equivalent re-check, and neither of its two callers re-filtered the result by organisation either — so a cross-org nesting edge that ever got into the database by some other means (a bug, a future code path, direct DB access) would surface a foreign organisation's group name under the wrong org's membership entry, unlike the project-role path, which would simply resolve to nothing. Not independently exploitable today (the same write-time check the project-role path also relies on already blocks it), but inconsistent with this codebase's own established posture on this exact class of check. Fixed by re-scoping the ancestor closure to the target `organization_id` inside `get_user_org_group_ids` itself, matching the sibling function. New test: `test_my_memberships_ancestor_closure_never_leaks_a_cross_org_group_name`, which — since the real API cannot construct this state at all — inserts a cross-org nesting edge directly via the DB session (the only way to reach it) and confirms the foreign organisation's group name never appears under the caller's own org entry.

**Found and fixed (minor, robustness not security): a malformed member id in a SCIM Group payload 500ed instead of being rejected cleanly.** `create_group`, `replace_group`, and `patch_group` all called `UUID(...)` directly on caller-supplied `members[].value` strings; a real IdP only ever echoes back an id this app itself issued, so this was never expected to matter in practice, but the request body is untyped (`dict[str, Any]`), so a malformed or hand-crafted request hit an uncaught `ValueError` and surfaced as a bare 500 rather than a clean skip — inconsistent with `_add_group_member`'s own existing tolerance for an unrecognised-but-well-formed id ("silently skip a member id that isn't a real org member"). Fixed with a shared `_parse_uuid` helper used at all three call sites. New test: `test_scim_group_patch_and_create_reject_malformed_member_ids_without_500ing`.

**Reviewed and found adequate, no changes needed**: `get_effective_project_roles`'s `ProjectVisibility.ORG_WIDE` grant (never implies manager/administrator/stakeholder; correctly excluded from `get_project_member_user_ids` and `can_manage_project_settings`, matching its own module-docstring claims) and `list_projects`'s parallel `accessible_ids` leg (queries `UserOrgRole` directly, the same source `get_effective_org_roles` itself reads — no discrepancy between the two). `add_org_group_member`'s existing checks (`_get_org_group_in_org` for the target group, `get_effective_org_roles` for a user being added, `would_create_org_group_cycle` plus same-org resolution for a nested group) and `_import_org_groups`'s two-pass nested-group import (already reuses `would_create_org_group_cycle` against live DB state, including edges added earlier in the same import — verified this holds correctly because SQLAlchemy's default autoflush makes each cycle check see prior adds within the same pass). `sync_org_groups_from_claims` — confirmed it runs after `auth_oidc.py::oidc_callback`'s existing `is_active`/`is_archived` check (a deactivated/banned account never reaches it, so no path to a new grant while banned) and only ever touches `user_id`-keyed `OrgGroupMember` rows, never `member_org_group_id` nesting edges. `routers/scim.py::require_scim_org`'s bearer-token resolution (hashed lookup via the same `hash_pat` used for PATs, 403 on a disabled organisation, 401 on anything else) and every `Users`/`Groups` handler's org-scoping (`_get_org_user`/`_get_org_group`, both confirmed to 404 rather than leak existence across organisations — covered by the existing `test_scim_cannot_access_another_organisations_resources`). `SCIM Users` provisioning's `is_banned` check on an already-existing matched account (a sixth instance of the class of gap the "Hardening pass on self-signup/invites/external-users" and "org merge-import" entries above describe — this one was already closed at build time, not something this pass had to find). The `download_file`/`get_branding` changes behind the original login-background-image bug fix: org logos and login-background images becoming genuinely public (no auth at all) is the fix's entire point, matches the same public-branding reasoning `get_org_login_info` already established, and avatars remain authenticated-only as before; a PAT-scoped caller can still freely read public-branding content regardless of its own scope, which is not a boundary crossing since the content is already public to a fully anonymous caller.

**SOC 2 documentation**: added SCIM bearer tokens as a documented credential type in `access-control-policy.md`'s Authentication section (item 9, mirroring item 8's PAT treatment — a third bearer-credential type, but organisation-scoped rather than user-scoped, since it authenticates an IdP integration with no user behind the request) and a new Provisioning/deprovisioning item (2a) covering the claims-based-vs-push-based (SCIM) sync split and cross-referencing the `is_banned` check above.

**Also found and fixed, while verifying: three pre-existing, unrelated Playwright races**, surfaced only because this pass's repeated pytest/Playwright runs put the shared dev stack under more concurrent load than usual. None touch groups/permissions/SCIM, and all three predate this session's overhaul entirely, but the user was explicit that a found issue gets fixed as part of the same piece of work, not documented and left — including the third one below, which this entry originally (wrongly) wrote off as "ordinary flakiness" after it passed on a single isolated retry, before the user corrected that reflex and asked for it to actually be fixed too.

`requirements-and-cr-filters.spec.ts`'s view-mode-persistence step clicked "List view" and immediately called `page.reload()` with no wait in between; `AuthContext.tsx`'s `setUiPreference` (`useUiPreference`'s backing implementation) fires its `PATCH /auth/me/preferences` fire-and-forget, with no await, so a reload that wins the race observes the pre-save state. `project-history.spec.ts`'s date-range-filter step had the same shape one line earlier: it filled the date field and asserted on the resulting `/changes` fetch with no explicit wait, relying purely on the assertion's own timeout to outlast the debounce+network round trip — inconsistent with the very next line in the same test step, which already waits for that endpoint explicitly. `org-security-controls.spec.ts`'s org-wide-2FA-enforcement step had the same shape again: it clicked into a project (triggering a `GET /api/v1/projects/{id}` that resolves 403 and drives the "2FA required" UI) and asserted on that UI immediately, with no wait for the request the assertion actually depends on. All three fixed the same way, mirroring the pattern `preferences-and-theme.spec.ts`'s "pronouns save and persist" step already established for this exact class of race: wrap the triggering action in `Promise.all([page.waitForResponse(...), action])` so the test only proceeds once the server call it depends on has actually resolved. The `org-security-controls.spec.ts` fix was re-verified with three consecutive isolated runs (not just one) specifically because the earlier single-retry-then-move-on approach is what produced the wrong call the first time.

**Verified**: targeted run of the seven affected/new test files (`test_scim.py`, `test_my_memberships.py`, `test_org_group_nesting.py`, `test_rbac.py`, `test_access_review.py`, `test_oidc_provisioning.py`) — 88 passed. Full backend suite run four times independently against a freshly wiped test database each time (the first two attempts were run in parallel with each other by mistake, producing a large false-failure batch from the resulting DB collision — a self-inflicted artifact of this pass's own verification process, not a product defect; the remaining runs, each alone, were clean): 483 passed (480 + 3 new), 0 regressions, every time. `ruff check` clean. Full 50-spec Playwright suite, run against freshly reseeded data at each stage: 48 passed / 2 failed before any race fix (`project-history.spec.ts`, `requirements-and-cr-filters.spec.ts`); 49 passed / 1 failed after fixing those two (`org-security-controls.spec.ts`, root-caused rather than accepted as flaky per the correction above); **50 passed, 0 failed** on the final run after fixing all three.

## Project statuses, typed bidirectional requirement links, and requirement actions

User request (backend half of an approved plan; frontend is a separate follow-up pass): three new capabilities — org-definable project statuses, org-extensible bidirectional requirement links (replacing the previous fixed `RequirementLinkType` enum), and requirement actions (a review/test task with its own identity, shareable across multiple requirements). Per the user's explicit direction and `docs/requirements.md` being authoritative and off-limits to the agent, the design rationale lives here rather than there.

**Org-definable project statuses (`ProjectStatusDefinition`)**: every organisation is seeded with 4 defaults (Proposed/Active/Abandoned/Completed) at creation; `Project.status_id` is `NOT NULL` with no `ON DELETE` action (implicit `RESTRICT`). This is deliberate double coverage, not redundancy for its own sake: the app layer already refuses to delete an in-use status (409, naming the affected count) and refuses to leave an organisation with zero statuses (409, so a project's status picker/default can never end up empty) — the DB-level `RESTRICT` is a second backstop against any future code path that forgets the app-layer check, since silently orphaning a `NOT NULL` foreign key would corrupt data far worse than a delete simply failing.

**Typed, bidirectional, org-extensible requirement links (`RequirementLinkTypeDefinition`)**: replaces the fixed `RequirementLinkType` enum (`relates_to`/`depends_on`/`derived_from`) with an org-definable table storing a `forward_name`/`reverse_name` pair per type (e.g. "Derives from" / "Is the source of"; a symmetric relationship like "Related to" just repeats the same name in both columns), seeded with 12 defaults per organisation and freely extensible beyond that. A client-side naming-convention heuristic (e.g. stripping/negating a word to guess the reverse direction) was rejected in favour of storing both names explicitly and resolving direction server-side, per request, in `routers/requirements.py::_link_to_out` — the requirement being viewed is compared against the link's `source_requirement_id` to decide `direction` (`"outgoing"`/`"incoming"`) and pick `forward_name` vs. `reverse_name`; a client-side guess would be both fragile (no reliable general rule for turning "Depends on" into "Is a dependency of") and duplicated logic the server already has to get right for validation anyway. `RequirementLink.link_type_id` is `NOT NULL` (no `RESTRICT`-vs-`CASCADE` ambiguity, since deleting a link type follows the same reassign-or-block rule as project statuses, never a bare cascade that would silently reinterpret a link's meaning).

**The enum-to-FK migration (`0012_project_statuses_link_types_actions.py`)**: the trickiest backfill in this change. `requirement_links.link_type` (old, 3-valued VARCHAR enum) is mapped to the new per-organisation `requirement_link_type_definitions` row via a `CASE rl.link_type WHEN 'relates_to' THEN 'Related to' ... END` join against the source requirement's own project's organisation, guarded by an `information_schema.columns` existence check so the whole block is a genuine no-op on a brand-new database (whose `0001_initial.py` builds straight from the *current* models, which never had a `link_type` column at all). The old 3-part unique constraint is located and dropped by inspecting `pg_constraint`/`pg_attribute` for column membership rather than a literal `DROP CONSTRAINT <name>`, because Postgres silently truncates that constraint's default-generated name past its 63-byte `NAMEDATALEN` limit — not worth trying to reproduce by hand. Two of the *new* schema's own unique constraints hit the same 63-byte limit (`requirement_link_type_definitions(organization_id, forward_name)` and `requirement_links(source_requirement_id, target_requirement_id, link_type_id)`), so both models were given an explicit short `name=` in their `UniqueConstraint(...)` — the same fix this codebase already established for this exact class of problem in migration `0009` (org-group nesting) — rather than leaving Postgres to truncate an 78-/66-byte auto-generated name unpredictably, which would have made a legacy-database migration's constraint name a fragile guess.

**Requirement actions (`RequirementAction`)**: a required task (review, test, etc.) gets its own project-scoped identity — its own `unique_code`, generated the same way `Requirement.unique_code` is, via a new `Project.next_action_seq` counter — rather than belonging to exactly one requirement, so `RequirementActionLink` is a genuine many-to-many join: one action (e.g. a single wind-tunnel stability test) can satisfy several requirements at once, and unlinking it from one requirement never deletes it or affects any other requirement still linked to it. `RequirementActionOutcome` (pending/completed/failed) stays a small fixed enum rather than a fourth org/project-configurable definition table alongside statuses/link-types/action-types — nothing in the request asks this vocabulary to vary per org/project, and every comparable lifecycle vocabulary already in this codebase (`RequirementReviewOutcome`, `ChangeRequestStatus`) is a fixed enum too. `completed_at`/`completed_by` stamp the moment `outcome_status` first leaves `PENDING` (`services/actions.py::apply_outcome_transition`) and clear again if reverted back to `PENDING`, mirroring `uncomplete_requirement`'s existing "revert a mistake" pattern. An action's own discussion thread and file attachments reuse the existing generic `ReviewComment`/`CommentFile` machinery (`ReviewTargetType.ACTION`, a third target type alongside requirement and change request) and a `RequirementActionFile` table structurally identical to `RequirementFile`, rather than a fourth parallel comment/attachment system.

**Action types are project-scoped, not org-scoped** (`ActionTypeDefinition`, matching `CustomFieldDefinition`'s existing project-scoping) — an explicit product decision made with an eye toward a possible future where projects can nest. No nested-project mechanism exists today and none was built here; the only concession made for that possible future is keeping this a plain per-project FK rather than baking a flat org-wide list in anywhere, so a later "fall back to the parent project's action types" resolution could be layered on top of this shape without a schema rework.

**Shared rename/delete/reassignment rules across all three definition tables** (`services/definitions.py::delete_definition_with_reassignment`, used identically by `ProjectStatusDefinition`, `RequirementLinkTypeDefinition`, and `ActionTypeDefinition`): renaming is a plain name update with zero cascading effect (every reference points at the row's id, never its name); a scope (organisation for statuses/link-types, project for action-types) must always retain at least one row, since the referencing FK is `NOT NULL` and a creation flow needs something to default to; deleting a row currently referenced by other rows requires an explicit `reassign_to_id` (validated same-scope, not-self) rather than a flat block or a silent cascade — the admin gets to choose where affected rows land, and both the reassignment and the subsequent delete are logged as separate audit events in one transaction. This one shared implementation, rather than three near-identical ad-hoc ones, is also why `_move_ordered` was extracted out of `routers/projects.py` into `services/ordering.py::move_ordered` — the same up/down swap logic three more reorder endpoints now needed.

**Reuse over duplication, applied consistently**: `services/actions.py::get_requirement_action_in_project`/`action_to_out` and the `services/definitions.py` helpers above are the two clearest instances, but the same principle shaped several smaller choices — `routers/actions.py` has its own small `_require_edit_role`/`CAN_EDIT_ROLES` rather than importing `routers/requirements.py`'s private copies (this codebase has no precedent for one router importing another's private helpers; the two IDOR-guard/output-builder functions above went into a service module specifically because *they* are needed by two different routers, `routers.actions` and `routers.requirements`'s new requirement<->action linking section).

**Existing code updated for the schema change**: `services/project_export.py` (bundle export/import) now matches a requirement link's type by `forward_name` within the *target* organisation rather than by enum value — like every other cross-tenant reference in a bundle (users by email, custom fields by name), a source-deployment link-type id means nothing on import; a bundle link whose type has no match in the target organisation is skipped with a warning rather than guessed. `services/requirement_csv.py`'s CSV export renders a link's forward name the same way. `routers/projects.py::create_project`/`services/templates.py::clone_project` both now assign `status_id` (defaulting to the organisation's first status; a cloned project never inherits the *template's own current* status, since that's lifecycle state, not portable configuration — same treatment `clone_project` already gives stages/baselines) and seed default action types — freshly for a from-scratch project, copied from the template for a cloned one (matching how custom field definitions are already copied).

**Verified**: full backend suite, run against the real Postgres test database per `backend/tests/conftest.py`'s established convention — 531 passed, 0 failed (483 pre-existing + 48 new, across 6 new test files covering create/list/reorder/rename/delete for all three definition tables including the full §4.0 rename/reorder/minimum-count/reassignment/cross-scope-IDOR matrix, directional `display_name` resolution from both ends of a link, action CRUD/archive/outcome-transition-stamping/comments/file-attachments, and multi-requirement action sharing/unlink-doesn't-delete) plus 2 pre-existing tests updated for the new required `link_type_id` field. Also found and fixed along the way: two pre-existing tests in `test_invites_and_external_users.py` that constructed `Project(...)` rows directly via the ORM (bypassing the API) started failing a `NOT NULL` constraint on `status_id` once that column became mandatory — fixed by seeding default statuses and passing an explicit `status_id`, the same pattern every other direct-ORM `Project` construction in this change now follows. `test_schema_migrations_match_models.py` passes (the Alembic-produced schema matches the models exactly, including the two explicitly-named unique constraints above). `alembic upgrade head` verified clean against a genuinely fresh database. The tricky enum-to-FK backfill's SQL was additionally verified in isolation against a hand-built minimal schema (`CASE 'depends_on' -> 'Depends on'` correctly resolves to the matching `requirement_link_type_definitions` row), since no pre-existing production-like database exists yet to replay the legacy upgrade path against end-to-end (this is still pre-release, per `0001_initial.py`'s own docstring). `ruff check` clean. Both `backend/scripts/seed_demo_data.py` and `seed_e2e_dataset.py` updated and run end-to-end against a live server: the demo dataset gets a custom "Supersedes" link type, 3 requirement links (2 default-typed, 1 custom-typed), one project moved to "Active" status, and 2 requirement actions (one completed review, one failed test, the failed one shared across two requirements); the E2E dataset gets a fixed custom link type, one fixed requirement link, and two fixed-title requirement actions (one completed, one pending) for a follow-up Playwright pass to write specs against.

## Project statuses, typed bidirectional requirement links, and requirement actions — frontend

Frontend half of the approved plan whose backend half is the entry immediately above: admin UI for the two new org-scoped definition tables and the one project-scoped one, a project status picker, a greenfield requirement-links UI, and a new requirement-actions feature (its own project-level pages plus a linking card on the requirement detail page). No schema/API changes; this pass consumes what the backend entry above already shipped.

**Org admin UI (`OrgAdminPage.tsx`) — Project Statuses and Link Types tabs**: both are new `CollapsibleSection`s built as a direct copy of the existing custom-fields admin pattern already on this page (add-row, inline rename, up/down reorder), with link types needing two name inputs per row (`forward_name`/`reverse_name`) instead of one. **Project Admin UI (`ProjectAdminPage.tsx`) — Action Types tab**: identical pattern, project-scoped instead of org-scoped, consistent with action types themselves being project-scoped per the backend entry's own reasoning.

**Delete/reassign UX, shared across all three lists**: rather than a bespoke confirm dialog per table, all three follow one interaction shape that mirrors the backend's own shared `reassign_to_id` contract exactly — click delete, call `DELETE` with no `reassign_to_id`; a plain success just removes the row; a 409 opens an inline reassignment picker showing the server's own in-use count message verbatim (never a generic re-derived one, so the picker can't drift out of sync with whatever count logic the backend uses) with a `<select>` of the scope's other rows, and only resubmits with `reassign_to_id` once something is chosen (`Confirm delete` stays disabled until then). The delete button itself is disabled — with a `title` tooltip, not silently — whenever the list has exactly one row left, since the backend's own minimum-one-remaining rule (§4.0 of the plan) guarantees that delete would 409 anyway; sending a request the UI already knows will fail would just be a slower way to show the same error. This is one interaction pattern implemented three times with different field names, not three separate designs, matching the backend's own single shared `services/definitions.py` helper.

**Project status picker (`ProjectAdminPage.tsx`)**: a plain `<select>` next to the pre-existing `visibility` control on the project settings tab, populated from the parent organisation's `project-statuses` list and bound to `status_id` on the existing `ProjectUpdate` PATCH call — no new save path, since `status_id` is just one more field on a form that already had a save button.

**Requirement links UI (`RequirementDetailPage.tsx`) — greenfield, no prior frontend consumer existed**: a "Links" card listing every link sorted by `display_name` then the other requirement's unique code, each row a clickable link to that requirement plus a remove button, and an inline add-link form (a requirement picker plus a `link_type_id` select rendering each type's `forward_name`, since the requirement currently being viewed is always the link's source from this page's point of view — the backend resolves `direction`/`display_name` per request, per the backend entry's reasoning, so this page never has to compute or guess which name applies). Neither the list nor the add-link form is gated by `requirement.is_locked`: links are traceability metadata about the requirement, not its governed content, the same distinction the backend entry already draws and the same one this codebase already applies to `ReviewComment` discussion threads.

**`FileAttachmentList.tsx` extraction**: the file-list-plus-upload-plus-remove block that used to live inline in `RequirementDetailPage.tsx`'s Attachments card is now a small shared component (`files`, `onUpload?`, `onRemove?`, `disabled?`, `emptyHint?`), used unchanged by both the requirement's own Attachments card and the new `ActionDetailPage.tsx`. `disabled`/`emptyHint` exist specifically for the requirement side's "approved, use a change request instead" lock (C-G-12); actions have no such lock concept, so `ActionDetailPage.tsx` simply never passes `disabled`, rather than the component needing to know which kind of entity it's attached to.

**Requirement actions UI — two new pages plus a card on the requirement detail page**: `ProjectActionsPage.tsx` is the project-wide home for actions (table of unique code/title/type/outcome/assignee/due date, filters mirroring the backend's own `outcome_status`/`action_type_id`/`include_archived` query params, and a "New Action" form) — it exists because a `RequirementAction` has its own project-scoped identity independent of any one requirement, per the backend entry, so it needs a browsing home that isn't nested under a single requirement. `ActionDetailPage.tsx` is the single-action view: editable fields, an outcome-status control, the reverse list of every requirement this action is linked to (computed client-side by walking the project's requirements and calling each one's `/actions` endpoint, since no bulk "which requirements link this action" endpoint exists and one wasn't worth adding for a single detail page), a reused `FileAttachmentList`, and a reused `CommentThread`. The **Actions card** on `RequirementDetailPage.tsx` covers the requirement-side view of the same relationship: a linked-actions list with unlink, a "link existing action" picker, and a "create and link" inline form that posts to the backend's own `create-and-link` endpoint in one round trip rather than two separate requests. New routes `/projects/:projectId/actions` and `/projects/:projectId/actions/:actionId` in `App.tsx`, plus an "Actions" nav-rail entry in `Layout.tsx`.

**No new API-client abstraction**: per this codebase's existing convention (no per-entity wrapper functions in `api/client.ts`), every new page calls `api.get/post/patch/delete` directly with literal URL strings, matching every other page in the app — `api/client.ts` itself needed no changes for this pass.

**Verified**: `cd frontend && npm run build` (`tsc -b && vite build`) clean, 0 TypeScript errors. `npx eslint .` clean — 5 warnings, identical in count and location to the pre-existing baseline on `main` (all in untouched `context/BrandingContext.tsx`/`context/ThemeContext.tsx`, `react-refresh/only-export-components`; none in any new or changed file). `npx storybook build` succeeded; all 16 new story entries (`FileAttachmentList` x7, `ActionDetailPage` x5, `ProjectActionsPage` x5, plus the extended `OrgAdminPage`/`ProjectAdminPage`/`RequirementDetailPage` story sets covering delete-disabled-at-last-row, delete-in-use-opens-reassign-picker, populated Links/Actions cards, add-link, and create-and-link-action) are present in the built `index.json`. Docker Compose dev/test stack (`tests/container/`) rebuilt and recreated per this repo's "containers don't bind-mount source" rule; backend suite re-run against it — 531 passed, 0 failed, confirming the backend entry's own numbers still hold unchanged. Full Playwright suite run against a freshly reset stack (`docker compose down -v && up -d --build`, both `seed_e2e_dataset.py` and `seed_demo_data.py` re-run against the clean database) exactly once: **54 passed, 0 failed** — all 4 new specs (`org-admin-project-statuses-and-link-types.spec.ts`, `project-admin-action-types.spec.ts`, `requirement-actions.spec.ts`, `requirement-links.spec.ts`) plus the full 50-spec pre-existing suite, none regressed by the `RequirementLink`/`Project` type-shape changes touching `RequirementDetailPage.tsx`. (An earlier run of the full suite, done without resetting the database between the standalone new-spec run and the full-suite run, showed 4 failures; investigation traced every one to the mutating new specs — add/rename/delete operations — colliding with state they'd already mutated on the prior pass, not to any application defect, and the clean re-run above confirms it.) `README.md` updated with a short mention of links/actions/statuses in the existing requirements-workflow paragraph, not a rewrite. `docs/solution-architecture.md` was already updated by the backend pass (new ER diagram sections); no further architectural change here since this pass is UI-only.

## PR #8 CI failure: `npm ci` rejected a lockfile that installed fine locally, and closing the Node-version gap between local dev and CI/Docker for good

CI's `frontend` job failed on `npm ci` with `npm error Missing: vite@8.2.1 from lock file` (also `fsevents@2.3.3`), even though `frontend/package-lock.json` had been committed straight after a locally-successful `npm install`/`npm run build`. Reproduced exactly by running `npm ci` against the committed lockfile inside a `node:20` container (matching `ci.yml`'s then-`node-version: "20"`, npm 10.8.2) — it failed there too, while the same lockfile installed cleanly with the local machine's npm 11.16 (bundled with a locally-installed Node 24, via `nvm`). Root cause: npm 11 resolved `@vitest/browser`'s and `@vitest/browser-playwright`'s nested `vite`/`fsevents` optional dependencies differently than npm 10.8.2 does, and wrote a lockfile missing the nested `node_modules/@vitest/browser/node_modules/vite` (and `.../browser-playwright/...`) entries that npm 10.8.2 expects — a lockfile that was complete and correct for npm 11, not corrupt, just wrong for the npm version CI and `frontend/Dockerfile` (then `node:20-alpine`) actually used. The user reported this as a *recurring* issue specifically after agent sessions, meaning previous passes had almost certainly hit the same class of drift; they fixed the immediate break themselves (`11c6a6d`, `5c54c4a` — regenerating `package-lock.json`, byte-identical to this investigation's own repro/regeneration) while this investigation was still in progress, leaving the recurrence-prevention work below.

**First attempt — pin local dev down to CI's Node 20 — abandoned per user feedback.** Initial approach added `frontend/.nvmrc` (`20`) and a `frontend/scripts/docker-npm-install.sh` that regenerated the lockfile inside `node:20-alpine` via Docker, plus an enforced Docker-based pre-commit hook. The user objected to requiring Docker just to commit a change, and separately asked whether CI could instead move to a later Node version — Node 20 was at/near its LTS end-of-life anyway, while this machine's (and presumably other agent sessions') default `nvm` Node was already 24. Regenerating under a stale `node_modules` from an *earlier* Docker attempt (bind-mounting a temp dir that still had a prior run's `node_modules`) silently reused macOS-only `@rollup/rollup-darwin-x64` and produced a lockfile missing every Linux rollup variant — worth remembering if a Docker-based regeneration is ever revisited: always use an isolated dir with *no* pre-existing `node_modules`, never a bind-mounted host directory another platform's install has already touched.

**Final fix — raise CI/Docker to Node 24 to match the environment agents already default to, instead of forcing every environment down to an older pin.** `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (`frontend` and `backend-and-e2e` jobs) and `frontend/Dockerfile` now use Node 24; `frontend/.nvmrc` and `package.json`'s new `"engines": {"node": "24.x"}` pin the same for local use. This makes the *local* npm 11.x that caused the original break the version CI now runs too, closing the gap from the other direction and removing Docker from the loop entirely: `frontend/scripts/sync-lockfile.sh` (replacing the abandoned `docker-npm-install.sh`) is now the required way to add/remove/bump a frontend dependency — it refuses to run under the wrong Node major version (checked against `.nvmrc`), reinstalls from a clean `node_modules`, and re-verifies with `npm ci`, all locally, no container. A tracked `.githooks/pre-commit` (wired up automatically via a `postinstall` script in `frontend/package.json` that sets `core.hooksPath` — a no-op, swallowed by `2>/dev/null || true`, in `frontend/Dockerfile`'s build where `.git` doesn't exist) runs `npm ci --dry-run` — non-destructive, writes nothing to disk — whenever `frontend/package.json` or `package-lock.json` is staged, and blocks the commit if they're out of sync. [CLAUDE.md](../CLAUDE.md) ("Frontend Dependency Changes") and [docs/development.md](development.md) ("Changing frontend dependencies") both document this so it reproduces automatically for future agent sessions.

**Verified**: `npm ci`/`npm run lint`/`npm run build` all pass under a fresh `node:24-alpine` container (matching the updated Dockerfile/CI exactly) from a clean checkout — lint shows the same 5 pre-existing warnings as the baseline entry above, build clean; `tests/playwright/package-lock.json` (also now installed with Node 24 in CI) verified with a clean `npm ci` too. The `postinstall` wiring was confirmed to actually set `core.hooksPath` after a plain `npm install`, to no-op harmlessly under `node:24-alpine` with no `.git` present (Dockerfile build path), and the hook itself was exercised both directly and via a real `git commit` against a deliberately desynced `package.json` — blocked with exit 1 and no commit created, then passed cleanly once reverted. Files changed: `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `frontend/Dockerfile`, `frontend/package.json` (`engines` + `postinstall`), `frontend/package-lock.json`, `frontend/.nvmrc`, `frontend/scripts/sync-lockfile.sh` (new), `.githooks/pre-commit` (new), `CLAUDE.md`, `docs/development.md` — no application code touched.

## Mobile nav-rail collapse toggle was inert (reported: "shown but does not do anything")

Root cause was a pre-existing, deliberate responsive rule in `frontend/src/styles/theme.css` (`@media (max-width: 860px)`, added when the rail/sidebar responsive layout was built): below 860px there isn't room for a full rail, content column, and the 240px filter/activity sidebar side by side, so the rule force-collapses `.nav-rail:not(.nav-rail-icons)` to icon-only width unconditionally. `Layout.tsx`'s expand/collapse button (`railCollapsed` state, persisted server-side via `useUiPreference`) still toggled correctly and re-rendered the class/icon/label, but the CSS override made both states render identically at that width — the button was fully functional, just visibly doing nothing, which reads as broken. This is in tension with `docs/requirements.md` U-P-02 ("fully responsive... including mobile devices").

**Fix**: added a `nav-rail-toggle-row` class to the toggle's wrapping `<div>` and hid it inside the existing 860px media query in `theme.css`, rather than changing the force-collapse behavior itself — the rail's forced icon-only layout below that width is intentional and still needed, only the now-inert control needed to go. A true off-canvas mobile drawer (so the rail can be fully hidden, not just icon-width, on phones) was scoped out as a larger redesign and flagged separately rather than folded into this fix.

**Verified**: new spec `tests/playwright/tests/e2e-workflows/mobile-nav-rail.spec.ts` — asserts the toggle is hidden at 700px width and visible again at 1280px, against a rebuilt/recreated `tests/container` frontend image. Passed. `golden-path.spec.ts` re-run clean (50/50 unaffected by the CSS-only change). Files changed: `frontend/src/components/Layout.tsx`, `frontend/src/styles/theme.css`, `tests/playwright/tests/e2e-workflows/mobile-nav-rail.spec.ts` (new).

## Two more bugs found during the same UX audit's deeper pass: a mobile card-grid overflow, and a dead-end 2FA login on org-branded pages

A follow-up, deeper round of the same UX audit (user asked to "go deeper" after the initial pass above) turned up two more small, self-contained bugs alongside a much larger set of design-consistency findings that stayed in the audit document rather than becoming code changes yet. Per this repo's own policy, a found bug gets fixed as part of the same pass rather than deferred — both were small and unambiguous enough to fix immediately, unlike the broader redesign work the audit is still gathering sign-off on.

**Mobile card-grid overflow**: `RequirementsPage.tsx`, `ChangeRequestsPage.tsx`, `ProjectListPage.tsx`, and `FavouritesPage.tsx` all sized their tile/card views with a fixed `gridTemplateColumns: "repeat(auto-fill, minmax(280px, 1fr))"`. Below ~431px viewport width the collapsed nav rail (56px) plus `.app-content`'s own 24px-each-side padding leaves less than 280px for the grid itself (e.g. 375 − 56 − 24 − 24 = 271px), so the single column that still fit was forced wider than its container — a reproducible ~9px of horizontal overflow and a page-level horizontal scrollbar on an iPhone SE/mini-class (375px) viewport. Fixed by clamping the track minimum to `minmax(min(280px, 100%), 1fr)` in all four files, so it never exceeds whatever width is actually available — a one-line change per file, no visual difference at any normal desktop/tablet width. `ProjectOverviewPage.tsx`'s own card grid already used a smaller `minmax(240px, 1fr)` and was never affected, left unchanged.
